Validity of Prandtl’s boundary layer from the Boltzmann theory
Chanwoo Kim, Trinh T. Nguyen

Abstract
We justify Prandtl equations and higher order Prandtl expansion from the hydrodynamic limit of the Boltzmann equations. Our fluid data is of the form shear flow , plus κ \sqrt{\kappa} order term in analytic spaces in x ∥ ∈ 𝕋 2 x_{\parallel}\in\mathbb{T}^{2} and Sobolev in x 3 ∈ ℝ + x_{3}\in\mathbb{R}_{+} . This work is the first to rigorously justify the Prandtl equations from the hydrodynamic limits of the Boltzmann equations. The novelty lies in obtaining estimates for the linearized Boltzmann equation with a diffusive boundary condition around a Prandtl layer shear flow in analytic spaces. The key techniques involve delicate commutator estimates and the use of local conservation law.



1
Introduction

In this paper, we consider the Boltzmann equation

\partial_{t}F+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x}F=\frac{1}{\kappa\varepsilon^{2}}Q(F,F),

(1.1)

subject to the boundary condition

F(t,x,\xi)=c_{\mu}\mu_{0}(\xi)\int_{n(x)\cdot\tilde{\xi}>0}F(t,x,\tilde{\xi})\,|n(x)\cdot\tilde{\xi}|\,d\tilde{\xi},\quad(x,\xi)\in\{\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}:n(x)\cdot\xi<0\},

(1.2)

where the bilinear hard-sphere collision operator is defined by

Q(F,G)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{|\omega|=1}|(\xi-\xi_{\star})\cdot\omega|\left\{F(\xi^{\prime})G(\xi^{\prime}_{\star})+G(\xi^{\prime})F(\xi^{\prime}_{\star})-F(\xi)G(\xi_{\star})-G(\xi)F(\xi_{\star})\right\}d\omega\,d\xi_{\star},

(1.3)

and
c_{\mu}>0
is a normalization constant given in
(
3.38
)
. The velocities
(\xi,\xi_{\star})
denote pre-collisional velocities, while
(\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}_{\star})
denote post-collisional velocities.

Understanding the hydrodynamic limit of
(
1.1
)
–
(
1.2
)
in the regime
\varepsilon,\kappa\to 0
is a longstanding and fundamental problem in kinetic theory, dating back to Hilbert’s sixth problem
[
41
]
.

The weak convergence of fluid moments was formally established in
[
6
]
and later rigorously justified for DiPerna–Lions renormalized solutions in
[
29
]
(see also
[
4
,
28
,
5
,
63
,
69
,
78
,
50
,
49
]
). Significant progress on the closeness of Hilbert expansions to solutions of the compressible Euler equations can be found in
[
9
,
61
,
76
,
82
]
, while further developments include shock profiles
[
84
]
, contact discontinuities
[
42
]
, vortex solutions
[
53
]
, low-regularity Euler flows
[
52
]
, relativistic regimes
[
81
]
, and steady solutions
[
16
,
15
,
19
,
20
,
2
,
3
,
17
,
18
]
. We also refer to
[
34
]
for the torus setting and
[
48
,
10
]
for half-space results.

A central open question is the hydrodynamic limit near singular fluid solutions in bounded domains, where classical Boltzmann solutions may cease to be
C^{1}
even for smooth convex boundaries. In particular, the derivation of Prandtl-type boundary layers from the Boltzmann equation remains one of the most fundamental and challenging problems in the field. Three major obstacles arise: (1) the Prandtl equations only asymptotically approximate—but do not exactly solve—the Navier–Stokes equations near the boundary; (2) boundary layer instabilities arise even at the linearized level; and (3) there is a lack of general regularity theory for the Boltzmann equation in domains with boundaries, especially under physical boundary conditions such as diffuse reflection.
These challenges originate from the singular behavior of the transport term
\varepsilon^{-1}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x}
acting on Maxwellians of the form
e^{-|\xi-\varepsilon U|^{2}}
where U denotes the fluid velocities. Similar difficulties persist in vortex-dominated regimes, highlighting the deep interplay between kinetic singularities and fluid instabilities—one of the most important open frontiers in modern kinetic theory.

On the fluid side, a classical and fundamental question in mathematical fluid dynamics is to understand the behavior of solutions to the Navier–Stokes equations in the vanishing viscosity limit. The incompressible Navier–Stokes equations are given by

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u^{\kappa}+u^{\kappa}\cdot\nabla u^{\kappa}+\nabla p^{\kappa}

\displaystyle=\eta_{0}\kappa\Delta u^{\kappa},\quad x\in\Omega,

(1.4)

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot u^{\kappa}

\displaystyle=0,

where
u^{\kappa}(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
denotes the velocity field,
p^{\kappa}\in\mathbb{R}
the pressure,
\kappa>0
the kinematic viscosity, and
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
the fluid domain. In the presence of boundaries, the classical no-slip condition is imposed:

u^{\kappa}|_{\partial\Omega}=0,

(1.5)

which prescribes that the fluid adheres to the boundary, with both tangential and normal components vanishing on
\partial\Omega
.

Formally setting
\kappa=0
yields the incompressible Euler equations, i.e.,
(
1.4
)
with
\kappa=0
, subject to the impermeability condition

u^{0}\cdot n|_{\partial\Omega}=0,

(1.6)

where
n
denotes the outward unit normal to
\partial\Omega
. In contrast to
(
1.5
)
, the Euler boundary condition permits tangential slip along the boundary, leading to a boundary layer mismatch between Navier–Stokes and Euler flows as
\kappa\to 0
.

To resolve this mismatch, Prandtl in 1904
[
77
]
proposed a boundary layer corrector. In the canonical half-space setting
(x_{\parallel},x_{3})\in\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
, the approximate solution takes the form

u^{\kappa}_{\text{app}}(t,x_{\parallel},x_{3})=u^{E}(t,x_{\parallel},x_{3})+u^{P}\left(t,x_{\parallel},\frac{x_{3}}{\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}}\right),

which satisfies the no-slip condition
u^{\kappa}_{\text{app}}|_{x_{3}=0}=0
. This leads to the celebrated Prandtl boundary layer theory, whose rigorous justification has since attracted extensive attention.

The Prandtl equations are locally well-posed for analytic data in
x_{\parallel}

[
79
,
65
,
58
]
, for monotonic shear flows
[
70
,
1
,
55
]
, and for Gevrey-class data
[
24
,
12
,
62
]
. In the absence of such structural conditions, however, the equations are known to be ill-posed in Sobolev spaces
[
22
,
25
,
37
]
. Even when the Prandtl expansion is well-posed, controlling the remainder
v:=u^{\kappa}-u^{\kappa}_{\text{app}}
, which also satisfies homogeneous Dirichlet boundary conditions, poses significant difficulties. The energy estimate for
v
contains the convection term

\int_{\Omega}(v\cdot\nabla u^{\kappa}_{\text{app}})\cdot v,

which becomes problematic due to the sharp boundary gradients:

\nabla u^{\kappa}_{\text{app}}\sim\kappa^{-1/2},

indicating an
O(\kappa^{-1/2})
blow-up in the
\kappa\to 0^{+}
limit. This large vorticity near the boundary is a key source of instability, and whether the boundary layer remains confined or propagates into the interior remains a central and delicate question.

In his seminal work
[
51
]
, Kato established a necessary and sufficient condition for the convergence

u^{\kappa}\to u^{0}\quad\text{in}\quad L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))

(1.7)

as
\kappa\to 0
, namely,

\kappa\int_{0}^{T}\int_{d(x,\partial\Omega)\lesssim\kappa}|\nabla u^{\kappa}|^{2}\,dx\,dt\to 0.

(1.8)

Despite the localization of the condition to a vanishingly thin boundary layer of size
\kappa
, verifying
(
1.8
)
remains an open challenge. Importantly, it reveals that any failure of
(
1.7
)
must involve subtle concentration phenomena at the boundary, beyond what is captured by formal Prandtl expansions, since
u^{\kappa}_{\text{app}}
does satisfy
(
1.8
)
.

Sammartino and Caflisch
[
79
,
80
]
rigorously justified the Prandtl expansion and the inviscid limit
(
1.7
)
for analytic initial data in the half-space. Maekawa
[
68
]
established the inviscid limit under the assumption that the initial vorticity vanishes near the boundary. For Euler shear flows that are monotonic and concave, the inviscid limit is known to hold for Gevrey-regular perturbations of size
O(\kappa^{1/2+})

[
23
]
.

Progress under additional symmetry or structural assumptions can be found in
[
66
,
67
,
26
]
for symmetric flows,
[
72
,
73
,
71
]
for channel and pipe geometries, and
[
64
,
27
]
for linearized problems. In the absence of any structural assumptions, the inviscid limit has only been established for analytic data near the boundary
[
74
,
59
,
83
,
60
,
56
,
7
,
75
]
.

For general smooth data, the formation of boundary vorticity and associated instabilities can lead to multi-scale phenomena that invalidate the Prandtl expansion. This mechanism, rigorously described in
[
30
,
33
,
31
,
32
]
, illustrating that small-scale vorticity generation can obstruct the inviscid limit
[
8
,
11
,
14
]
. In the stationary setting, significant progress has been made on rigorous boundary layer expansions; see, for instance,
[
38
,
43
,
44
,
47
,
36
,
35
,
45
,
46
,
21
,
13
,
39
,
40
]
.

The goal of the present work is to rigorously justify the Prandtl boundary layer expansion directly from the Boltzmann equation, without passing through the intermediate Navier–Stokes equations. This direct approach is particularly significant: while the Prandtl equations are formally derived from the Navier–Stokes system with no-slip boundary conditions, the validity of this derivation remains delicate due to instability and ill-posedness issues. Moreover, the Navier–Stokes equations themselves do not provide a uniformly valid description of fluid motion in the vanishing viscosity limit, especially near boundaries where sharp velocity gradients and boundary layers form. As a result, the justification of reduced models such as the Prandtl equations—or more generally, asymptotic fluid approximations—from kinetic theory becomes essential. The Boltzmann equation, equipped with diffusive boundary conditions, offers a more physically grounded framework that naturally incorporates microscopic boundary interactions without invoking the controversial no-slip assumption. In this work, we establish the hydrodynamic limit for a class of Prandtl shear flows under diffusive reflection, thereby resolving a longstanding open problem concerning singular fluid limits with large boundary velocity gradients. Our analysis yields the first rigorous derivation of the Prandtl equations from kinetic theory and introduces new techniques to capture boundary layer behavior in singular asymptotic regimes—a decisive step toward understanding this fundamental scaling limit.

To be more precise, we consider the Boltzmann equation
(
1.1
)
. The goal is to justify that the leading behavior of the solution
F
is given by

F=\mu+\varepsilon^{2}{\bf{f_{2}}}\sqrt{\mu}+\varepsilon\delta f\sqrt{\mu}

where
\mu+\varepsilon^{2}{\bf{f_{2}}}\sqrt{\mu}
is the approximate solution to the Boltzmann equation
(
1.1
)

built from the fluid equations and
\varepsilon\delta f\sqrt{\mu}
is the remainder. Here, the function
\mu
is the local Maxwellian corresponding the the velocity
U(t,x)
of the fluid, given by

\mu(t,x,\xi)=\mu_{0}(\xi-\varepsilon U).

(1.9)

Here,
\mu_{0}
is the global Maxwellian
\mu_{0}(\xi)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\frac{3}{2}}}e^{-\frac{|\xi|^{2}}{2}}
.
One can then write the equation for the remainder as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x}f+\frac{1}{\kappa{\varepsilon}^{2}}Lf+\frac{(\partial_{t}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})\sqrt{\mu}}{\sqrt{\mu}}f

(1.10)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\delta}\textbf{NS}(U)\cdot\varphi\sqrt{\mu}+\frac{\varepsilon}{\kappa\delta}\Gamma({\bf{f_{2}}},{\bf{f_{2}}})+\frac{\delta}{\kappa{\varepsilon}}\Gamma(f,f)+\frac{2}{\kappa}\Gamma({\bf{f_{2}}},f)+\text{other terms}.

Here,
\textbf{NS}(U)
is the Navier-Stokes applied to
U
, which will be specified in the next section.
Since
U
involves a fast scale variable
\frac{x_{3}}{\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}}
, we see that
\partial_{x_{3}}U\sim\kappa^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, and so one needs to deal with the singular term

\frac{(\partial_{t}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})\sqrt{\mu}}{\sqrt{\mu}}f\sim\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\frac{\xi_{3}\partial_{x_{3}}\sqrt{\mu}}{\sqrt{\mu}}\sim\partial_{x_{3}}U_{\parallel}\xi_{\parallel}\xi_{3}f\sim\kappa^{-\frac{1}{2}}\xi_{\parallel}\xi_{3}f.

The problem indeed also appears in the paper
[
48
]
. One of the crucial ideas of the authors in
[
48
]
is that
U
solve the
exact
Navier-Stokes equations, giving an error on the right hand side of
(
1.10
)
to be
O(\varepsilon)
. Hence, by the energy and Gronwall estimate, formally one gets

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\kappa^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}}+O(\varepsilon),

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon e^{\kappa^{-\frac{1}{2}}}.

This implies the hydrodynamic limits for the
Navier-Stokes
solutions
\textbf{NS}(U)=0
under the regime
\varepsilon e^{\kappa^{-\frac{1}{2}}}\to 0
. In our current work, since
U
does not solve the Navier-Stokes equations and only solve the Prandtl equations, which always leave a remainder in the Navier-Stokes equations, we can expect at most

\textbf{NS}(U)\lesssim\kappa^{N}

for some
N\geq 1
large, but finite. A rough
L^{2}
estimate for the equation
(
1.10
)
gives

\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\kappa^{N}e^{\kappa^{-\frac{1}{2}}},

which blows up when
\kappa\to 0^{+}
. In this paper, we settle this problem in the analytic framework for Prandtl shear flows. We consider
\Omega={\mathbb{T}}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
and denote

x=(x_{\parallel},x_{3}),\quad x_{\parallel}=(x_{1},x_{2})\in{\mathbb{T}}^{2},\quad x_{3}\geq 0.

We have the formal statement of the main theorem

Theorem 1.1
.

For any Prandtl layers of the form

\displaystyle U(t,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})

\displaystyle=(u_{e}^{0}(t,x_{3})+u_{p}^{0}\left(t,\frac{x_{3}}{\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}}\right)

\displaystyle+\begin{bmatrix}&\sum_{i=0}^{N}\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{i}\left(u_{e}^{i}(t,x_{\parallel},x_{3})+u_{p}^{i}(t,x_{\parallel},\frac{x_{3}}{\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}}\right)\\
&\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{i+1}\left(v_{e}^{i+1}(t,x_{\parallel},\frac{x_{3}}{\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}})+v_{p}^{i+1}(t,x_{\parallel},\frac{x_{3}}{\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}}\right)\end{bmatrix}

that is analytic in
\ x_{\parallel}\in{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
and analytic in time. There exists a solution
F
to the Boltzmann equations with the diffusive boundary condition such that

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left\|\frac{F-\mu}{\sqrt{\mu}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{x,\xi}}\to 0

as
\varepsilon,\kappa\to 0^{+}
, in the regime

\varepsilon\sim\kappa^{N}\quad\text{and}\quad N\gg 1.

Ideas of the proof

Our first ingredient is to build an approximate solution to the Boltzmann equation from the Prandtl equations. To this end,
we solve the Prandtl equations, which in turn solves the Navier-Stokes equations up to some high order
\kappa^{N}
. It is important that our Prandtl velocity satisfies the no-slip boundary condition, so that the local Maxwellian
\mu
(see
(
1.9
)
) matches the global Maxwellian
\mu_{0}
at the boundary.
Next, we use the Hilbert expansion

F=\mu+\varepsilon^{2}{\bf{f_{2}}}\sqrt{\mu}+\varepsilon\delta f\sqrt{\mu}

where
{\bf{f_{2}}}
is selected so that the remainder has small macroscopic part (see
(
3.11
)
), and
f
solves the remainder equation
(
1.10
)
.
Multiplying both sides by
f
and integrating, we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\left\|f(t)\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+O(1)\frac{1}{\kappa\varepsilon^{2}}\left\|({\mathbf{I}}-\mathbf{P})f\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{(\partial_{t}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})\sqrt{\mu}}{\sqrt{\mu}}|f|^{2}dxd\xi

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\delta}\left|\int_{\Omega}\textbf{NS}(U)\cdot bdx\right|+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\delta\sqrt{\kappa}}\left|\langle\Gamma({\bf{f_{2}}},{\bf{f_{2}}}),\frac{1}{\sqrt{\kappa}\varepsilon}({\mathbf{I}}-\mathbf{P})f\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle+\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{\kappa}}\left|\langle\Gamma(f,f),\frac{1}{\sqrt{\kappa}{\varepsilon}}({\mathbf{I}}-\mathbf{P})f\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|+\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{\kappa}}\left|\langle\Gamma({\bf{f_{2}}},f),\frac{1}{\sqrt{\kappa}{\varepsilon}}({\mathbf{I}}-\mathbf{P})f\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|.

Our first key observation is that

\int_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{(\partial_{t}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})\sqrt{\mu}}{\sqrt{\mu}}|f|^{2}dxd\xi=\partial_{x_{3}}U_{1}b_{1}b_{3}+\text{lower order terms}.

We note that
U_{1}
involves boundary layer fast variable
z=\frac{x_{3}}{\sqrt{\kappa}}
, therefore
\partial_{3}U_{1}\sim\kappa^{-1/2}
. To treat the term
\partial_{3}U_{1}b_{1}b_{3}
, we first note that
x_{3}\partial_{3}U_{1}=z\partial_{z}U_{1}\lesssim 1
, therefore

\int_{\Omega}\partial_{3}U_{1}b_{1}b_{3}dx=\int x_{3}\partial_{x_{3}}U_{1}\cdot b_{1}\cdot\frac{b_{3}}{x_{3}}\lesssim\|b_{1}\|_{L^{2}}\|\partial_{3}b_{3}\|_{L^{2}}.

Here, we use the Hardy inequality for
b_{3}
, thanks to the diffusive boundary condition (see Proposition
3.6
).
To treat the term
\partial_{3}b_{3}
, we use the key conservation law

\varepsilon\partial_{t}a+\partial_{1}b_{1}+\partial_{2}b_{2}+\partial_{3}b_{3}=-\frac{1}{\varepsilon\delta}(\nabla_{x}\cdot U)+\text{lower order terms}.

Thus we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\left\|f(t)\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+O(1)\frac{1}{\kappa\varepsilon^{2}}\left\|({\mathbf{I}}-\mathbf{P})f\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{(\partial_{t}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})\sqrt{\mu}}{\sqrt{\mu}}|f|^{2}dxd\xi

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\delta}\left|\int_{\Omega}\textbf{NS}(U)\cdot bdx\right|+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\delta\sqrt{\kappa}}\left|\langle\Gamma({\bf{f_{2}}},{\bf{f_{2}}}),\frac{1}{\sqrt{\kappa}\varepsilon}({\mathbf{I}}-\mathbf{P})f\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle+\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{\kappa}}\left|\langle\Gamma(f,f),\frac{1}{\sqrt{\kappa}{\varepsilon}}({\mathbf{I}}-\mathbf{P})f\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|+\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{\kappa}}\left|\langle\Gamma({\bf{f_{2}}},f),\frac{1}{\sqrt{\kappa}{\varepsilon}}({\mathbf{I}}-\mathbf{P})f\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|+\cdots.

Now in order to close the estimate for
L^{2}
, we need

\frac{1}{\delta}\left\|\textbf{NS}(U)\right\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}\delta}\left\|\nabla_{x}\cdot U\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 1.

To this end, we construct a high order approximation of the Navier-Stokes equations up to order
\kappa^{2N}
. Since we lose
\varepsilon\partial_{t},\partial_{x_{1}},\partial_{x_{2}}
of
f
in the energy estimate, we work with analyticity norms in these derivatives, see section
3.2
. Fix
\partial\in\{\varepsilon\partial_{t},\partial_{x_{1}},\partial_{x_{2}}\}
, we have a new commutator term
\frac{1}{\kappa\varepsilon^{2}}[L,\partial]f
in studying the equation for
\partial f
. To be more precise,
\partial f
solves

\left(\partial_{t}+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x}+\frac{1}{\kappa\varepsilon^{2}}L\right)(\partial f)=-\frac{1}{\kappa\varepsilon^{2}}[L,\partial]f+\text{other terms}.

By an explicit calculation, we see that

\frac{1}{\kappa\varepsilon^{2}}[L,\partial]f\sim\frac{1}{\sqrt{\kappa}}\partial U_{1}b_{3}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\kappa}\varepsilon}\langle({\mathbf{I}}-\mathbf{P})f,\hat{A}_{13}\rangle.

Since
U_{1}\sim u_{e}^{0}(t,x_{3})+u_{p}^{0}(t,\frac{x_{3}}{\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}})+O(\sqrt{\kappa})
, we have

\partial_{\parallel}U_{1}=\partial_{x_{\parallel}}\left\{u_{e}^{0}(t,x_{3})+u_{p}^{0}(t,\frac{x_{3}}{\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}})\right\}+O(\sqrt{\kappa})\sim\sqrt{\kappa}.

This gives us the formal estimate
\frac{1}{\kappa\varepsilon^{2}}\langle[L,\partial]f,\partial f\rangle\lesssim\left\|\partial f\right\|_{L^{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\kappa}\varepsilon}\left\|({\mathbf{I}}-\mathbf{P})f\right\|_{L^{2}}
. Finally, we construct the corresponding analytic fluid solutions and estimate their remainders in the analytic space, see Theorem
2.2
.

2
Analysis of the Euler and Prandtl equations

In this paper, we assume that the initial data is of the form

\displaystyle u|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=u_{e}^{0}(x_{3})+u_{p}^{0}\left(\frac{x_{3}}{\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{N}{(\eta_{0}\kappa)}^{\frac{i}{2}}\left(u_{e,0}^{i}(x_{\parallel},x_{3})+u_{p,0}^{i}\left(x_{\parallel},\frac{x_{3}}{\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}}\right)\right),

(2.1)

\displaystyle v|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}v_{e,0}^{1}(x_{\parallel},{x_{3}})+\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}{(\eta_{0}\kappa)}^{\frac{i+1}{2}}\left(v_{p,0}^{i+1}(x_{\parallel},\frac{x_{3}}{\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}})+v_{e,0}^{i+1}(x_{\parallel},{x_{3}})\right)

\displaystyle+{(\eta_{0}\kappa)}^{\frac{N+1}{2}}v_{p,0}^{N+1}(x_{\parallel},\frac{x_{3}}{\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}}),

and
\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{2}}u_{e,0}^{i}d{x_{\parallel}}=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{2}}v_{e,0}^{i}d{x_{\parallel}}=0
.
The Navier-Stokes equations on
\Omega=\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
reads

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+u\cdot\nabla_{x_{\parallel}}u+v\partial_{x_{3}}u+\nabla_{x_{\parallel}}p-\eta_{0}\kappa\Delta u=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v+u\cdot\nabla_{x_{\parallel}}v+v\partial_{x_{3}}v+\partial_{x_{3}}p-\eta_{0}\kappa\Delta v=0,

\displaystyle\nabla_{x_{\parallel}}\cdot u+\partial_{x_{3}}v=0.

Here we denote

U(t,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=(u(t,x_{\parallel},x_{3}),v(t,x_{\parallel},x_{3})),\qquad x_{\parallel}\in\mathbb{T}^{2},\quad x_{3}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}.

Define the stretched variable

z=\frac{x_{3}}{\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}}.

(2.2)

We build an approximation solution
U=(u_{a},v_{a})
of the form

\displaystyle u_{a}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{N}\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{i}\left(u_{e}^{i}(t,x_{\parallel},x_{3})+u_{p}^{i}(t,x_{\parallel},z)\right),

(2.3)

\displaystyle v_{a}

\displaystyle=v_{e}^{0}(t,x_{\parallel},{x_{3}})+\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}{\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}}^{i+1}\left(v_{p}^{i+1}(t,x_{\parallel},z)+v_{e}^{i+1}(t,x_{\parallel},{x_{3}})\right)+\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{N+1}v_{p}^{N+1}(t,x_{\parallel},z),

\displaystyle p_{a}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{N}\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{i}\left(p_{e}^{i}(t,x_{\parallel},{x_{3}})+p_{p}^{i}(t,x_{\parallel},z)\right).

We have the zero-order Euler equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u_{e}^{0}+(u_{e}^{0}\cdot\nabla_{x_{\parallel}}+v_{e}^{0}\partial_{x_{3}})u_{e}^{0}+\nabla_{x_{\parallel}}p_{e}^{0}=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v_{e}^{0}+(u_{e}^{0}\cdot\nabla_{x_{\parallel}}+v_{e}^{0}\partial_{x_{3}})v_{e}^{0}+\partial_{x_{3}}p_{e}^{0}=0,

\displaystyle\nabla_{x_{\parallel}}\cdot u_{e}^{0}+\partial_{x_{3}}v_{e}^{0}=0,\quad v_{e}^{0}|_{{x_{3}}=0}=0.

Due to the mismatch in the boundary condition, we add a Prandtl corrector

(u_{p}^{0}(t,x_{\parallel},z),\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}v_{p}^{1}(t,x_{\parallel},z)

given by

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u_{p}^{0}-\partial_{z}^{2}u_{p}^{0}+u_{p}^{0}\partial_{x}u_{e}^{0}+(u_{e}^{0}|_{x_{3}=0}+u_{p}^{0})\partial_{x_{\parallel}}u_{p}^{0}+(v_{e}^{1}|_{z=0}+v_{p}^{1}+z\partial_{x_{3}}v_{e}^{0}|_{x_{3}=0})\partial_{z}u_{p}^{0}=0,

\displaystyle v_{p}^{1}=-\int_{z}^{\infty}(\nabla_{x}\cdot u_{p}^{0})dz^{\prime},

\displaystyle u_{p}^{0}|_{z=0}=-u_{e}^{0}|_{{x_{3}}=0},\quad u_{p}^{0}|_{z=\infty}=0,

\displaystyle u_{p}^{0}|_{t=0}=u_{p,0}.

The new approximate solution does not satisfy the equation the boundary condition
v_{p}^{0}|_{x_{3}=0}=0
, hence one needs to introduce
(u_{e}^{1},v_{e}^{1})
solving

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u_{e}^{1}+(u_{e}^{1}\partial_{x_{\parallel}}+v_{e}^{1}\partial_{x_{3}})u_{e}^{0}+(u_{e}^{0}\partial_{x_{\parallel}}+v_{e}^{0}\partial_{x_{3}})u_{e}^{1}+\partial_{x_{\parallel}}p_{e}^{1}=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v_{e}^{1}+(u_{e}^{1}\partial_{x_{\parallel}}+v_{e}^{1}\partial_{x_{3}})v_{e}^{0}+(u_{e}^{0}\partial_{x_{\parallel}}+v_{e}^{0}\partial_{x_{3}})v_{e}^{1}+\partial_{x_{3}}p_{e}^{1}=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{\parallel}}u_{e}^{1}+\partial_{x_{3}}v_{e}^{1}=0,

\displaystyle(u_{e}^{1},v_{e}^{1})|_{t=0}=(u_{e,0}^{1},v_{e,0}^{1}),

\displaystyle v_{e}^{1}|_{{x_{3}}=0}=-v_{p}^{1}|_{z=0}.

For
0\leq m\leq N
, we have the linearized Euler equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u_{e}^{m}+(u_{e}^{0}\cdot\nabla_{x_{\parallel}}+v_{e}^{0}\partial_{x_{3}})u_{e}^{m}+(u_{e}^{m}\cdot\nabla_{x_{\parallel}}+v_{e}^{m}\partial_{x_{3}})u_{e}^{0}+\partial_{x_{\parallel}}p_{e}^{m}

(2.4)

\displaystyle=-\chi_{\{m\geq 1\}}\sum_{1\leq i\leq m-1}\left(u_{e}^{m-i}\partial_{x}u_{e}^{i}+v_{e}^{m-i}\partial_{x_{3}}u_{e}^{i}\right)+\chi_{\{m\geq 2\}}\Delta u_{e}^{m-2},

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v_{e}^{m}+(u_{e}^{m}\cdot\nabla_{x_{\parallel}}+v_{e}^{m}\partial_{x_{3}})v_{e}^{0}+(u_{e}^{0}\cdot\nabla_{x_{\parallel}}+v_{e}^{0}\partial_{x_{3}})v_{e}^{m}+\partial_{x_{3}}p_{e}^{m},

\displaystyle=-\chi_{\{m\geq 1\}}\sum_{1\leq i\leq m-1}\left(u_{e}^{m-i}\partial_{x}v_{e}^{i}+v_{e}^{m-i}\partial_{x_{3}}v_{e}^{i}\right)+\chi_{\{m\geq 2\}}\Delta v_{e}^{m-2},

\displaystyle\nabla_{x_{\parallel}}\cdot u_{e}^{m}+\partial_{x_{3}}v_{e}^{m}=0,\qquad v_{e}^{m}|_{x_{3}=0}=-v_{p}^{m}|_{z=0},\qquad 1\leq m\leq N.

The Prandtl equations
(u_{p}^{m},v_{p}^{m})
solves

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-\partial_{z}^{2})u_{p}^{m}+\left\{u_{p}^{m}\partial_{x_{\parallel}}u_{e}^{0}|_{{x_{3}}=0}+\partial_{x}u_{p}^{m}\left(u_{e}^{0}|_{{x_{3}}=0}+u_{p}^{0}\right)+\partial_{x_{\parallel}}u_{p}^{0}(u_{e}^{m}|_{{x_{3}}=0}+u_{p}^{m})\right\}

(2.5)

\displaystyle+(v_{e}^{1}|_{{x_{3}}=0}+z\partial_{x_{3}}v_{e}^{0}|_{{x_{3}}=0}+v_{p}^{1})\partial_{z}u_{p}^{m}

\displaystyle+(v_{e}^{m+1}|_{{x_{3}}=0}+v_{p}^{m+1})\partial_{z}u_{p}^{0}\cdot\chi_{\{m\leq N-1\}}+\chi_{\{1\leq m\leq N-1\}}\partial_{z}p_{p}^{m+1}=f_{p,\parallel}^{m},

\displaystyle u_{p}^{m}|_{z=0}=-u_{e}^{m}|_{x_{3}=0},\quad u_{p}^{m}|_{z=\infty}=0,\qquad 0\leq m\leq N,

\displaystyle v_{p}^{m+1}=-\int_{z}^{\infty}\nabla_{x_{\parallel}}\cdot u_{p}^{m}dz^{\prime},

where

\displaystyle f_{p,\parallel}^{m}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j+k=m\\
i\leq m-1\\
k\geq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}\partial_{x_{3}}^{k}\nabla_{x_{\parallel}}\cdot u_{e}^{j}|_{{x_{3}}=0}u_{p}^{i}-\sum_{0\leq i\leq m-1}\partial_{x_{\parallel}}u_{p}^{i}(u_{e}^{m-i}|_{{x_{3}}=0}+u_{p}^{m-i})

\displaystyle-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j+k=m\\
k\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}\partial_{x_{3}}^{k}u_{e}^{i}|_{{x_{3}}=0}\partial_{x_{\parallel}}u_{p}^{j}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i,j\leq N\\
k\geq 0\\
i+j+k=m\end{subarray}}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}\partial_{x_{3}}^{k+1}u_{e}^{j}|_{{x_{3}}=0}v_{p}^{i}

\displaystyle-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k+j=m+1\\
k\geq 1\\
0\leq j\leq m-1\end{subarray}}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}\partial_{x_{3}}^{k}v_{e}^{0}|_{{x_{3}}=0}\cdot\partial_{z}u_{p}^{j}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=m\\
0\leq i\leq m-1\\
0\leq j\leq m-1\\
\end{subarray}}(v_{e}^{i+1}|_{{x_{3}}=0}+v_{p}^{i+1})\partial_{z}u_{p}^{j}\cdot\chi_{\{m\leq N-1\}}

\displaystyle-\chi_{\{m\geq 2\}}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j+k=m\\
0\leq i\leq N-1\\
0\leq j\leq N\\
k\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}\partial_{x_{3}}^{k}v_{e}^{i+1}|_{{x_{3}}=0}\partial_{z}u_{p}^{j}+\chi_{\{m\geq 2\}}\Delta_{x_{\parallel}}u_{p}^{m-2}.

2.1
Analytic norms of the fluid equations

In this section, we first introduce the analytic norms for the fluid solutions, namely the approximate Prandtl solutions on the domain
\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
We adopt the multi-indices notation for the time-space derivatives:

\partial^{\alpha}=(\varepsilon\partial_{t})^{\alpha_{0}}\partial_{x_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\partial_{x_{2}}^{\alpha_{2}},\ \ \alpha=(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{3}_{0},

(2.6)

where
\mathbb{N}_{0}=\{0,1,2,\cdots\}
. We also denote

\langle\alpha\rangle=1+|\alpha|=1+\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2},\qquad\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3}.

Define, for
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
and
\partial^{\alpha}
as above,

\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(t)=\frac{\tau(t)^{|\alpha|}}{\alpha!}\langle\alpha\rangle^{9},\qquad\langle\alpha\rangle=1+|\alpha|

(2.7)

for a continuous function
\tau(t)=\tau_{0}-M_{0}t
for some constants
\tau_{0},M_{0}>0
depending only on the initial data.
For a function
f=f(t,x)
that only depends on
x\in\Omega
and
t\geq 0
, we define

\||f(t)\||_{\tau,p}=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{3}_{0}}\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(t)\left\|\partial^{\alpha}f(t)\right\|_{L^{p}_{x}}.

The existence and uniqueness of the Prandtl and Euler solutions in the analytic space
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\tau_{0},2}
and
\left\|\right\|_{\tau_{0},\infty}
are standard, and we refer the readers to
[
79
,
58
,
57
,
12
,
56
]
for the proof.

Theorem 2.1
.

Suppose the initial data
(u_{p}^{m},v_{p}^{m})|_{t=0}
and
(u_{e}^{m},v_{e}^{m})|_{t=0}
given in
(
2.1
)
is bounded in
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\tau_{0},2}\cap\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\tau_{0},\infty}
for
0\leq m\leq N
for some
\tau_{0}>0
, there exists a unique local solution in the space
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\tau_{0}/2,2}\cap\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\tau_{0}/2,\infty}
.

Our next goal is to show that the approximate solution gives a small error in the analytic spaces for
N
large. For
(u_{a},v_{a},p_{a})
defined in
(
2.3
)
, we define

\displaystyle\text{NS}_{\parallel}(u_{a})

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}u_{a}+(u_{a}\cdot\nabla_{x_{\parallel}}+v_{a}\partial_{x_{3}})u_{a}-{\eta_{0}\kappa}\Delta u_{a}+\nabla_{x_{\parallel}}p_{a},

\displaystyle\text{NS}_{x_{3}}(v_{a})

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}v_{a}+(u_{a}\cdot\nabla_{x_{\parallel}}+v_{a}\partial_{x_{3}})v_{a}-{\eta_{0}\kappa}\Delta v_{a}+\partial_{x_{3}}p_{a}.

\displaystyle\text{NS}_{\parallel}(u_{a})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j\geq N+1\\
i,j\leq N\end{subarray}}\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{i+j}\left(u_{e}^{i}\partial_{x_{\parallel}}u_{e}^{j}+v_{e}^{i}\partial_{x_{3}}u_{e}^{j}\right)

(2.8)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq i,j\leq N\\
k\geq 0\\
i+j+k\geq N+1\end{subarray}}\frac{\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{i+j+k}z^{k}}{k!}u_{p}^{i}\partial_{x_{3}}^{k}\partial_{x_{\parallel}}u_{e}^{j}|_{{x_{3}}=0}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq i,j\leq N\\
i+j\geq N+1\end{subarray}}\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{i+j}(u_{e}^{i}|_{{x_{3}}=0}+u_{p}^{i})\partial_{x_{\parallel}}u_{p}^{j}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq i,j\leq N\\
i+j+k\geq N+1\end{subarray}}\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{i+j+k}\frac{1}{k!}z^{k}\partial_{x_{3}}^{k}u_{e}^{i}|_{{x_{3}}=0}\partial_{x_{\parallel}}u_{p}^{j}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq i\leq N-1\\
1\leq j\leq N\\
k\geq 0\\
i+j+k\geq N\end{subarray}}\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{i+j+k+1}v_{p}^{i+1}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}\partial_{x_{3}}^{k+1}u_{e}^{j}(t,{x_{\parallel}},0)+\sum_{j=0}^{N}\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{N+1+j}v_{p}^{N+1}\partial_{x_{3}}u_{e}^{j}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k+j-1\geq N+1\\
0\leq j\leq N\\
k\geq 1\end{subarray}}\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{k+j-1}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}\partial_{x_{3}}^{k}v_{e}^{0}|_{{x_{3}}=0}\partial_{z}u_{p}^{j}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j\geq N+1\\
0\leq i\leq N-1\\
0\leq j\leq N\end{subarray}}\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{i+j}(v_{p}^{i+1}+v_{e}^{i+1}|_{{x_{3}}=0})\partial_{z}u_{p}^{j}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j+k\geq N+1\\
0\leq i\leq N-1\\
0\leq j\leq N\\
k\geq 1\end{subarray}}\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{i+j+k}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}\partial_{x_{3}}^{k}v_{e}^{i+1}|_{{x_{3}}=0}\partial_{z}u_{p}^{j}.

and

\displaystyle\text{NS}_{x_{3}}(v_{a})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j\geq N+1\\
0\leq i,j\leq N\end{subarray}}\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{i+j}\left(u_{e}^{i}\partial_{x_{\parallel}}v_{e}^{j}+v_{e}^{i}\partial_{x_{3}}v_{e}^{j}\right)

(2.9)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq i,j\leq N\\
k\geq 0\\
i+j+k\geq N+1\end{subarray}}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}u_{p}^{i}\partial_{x_{3}}^{k}\partial_{x_{\parallel}}v_{e}^{j}|_{{x_{3}}=0}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq i\leq N\\
0\leq j\leq N-1\\
i+j\geq N\end{subarray}}\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{i+j+1}(u_{e}^{i}|_{{x_{3}}=0}+u_{p}^{i})\partial_{x_{\parallel}}v_{p}^{j+1}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\leq N,j\leq N-1,k\geq 1\\
i+j+k\geq N\end{subarray}}\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{i+j+k+1}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}\partial_{x_{3}}^{k}u_{e}^{i}|_{{x_{3}}=0}\partial_{x_{\parallel}}v_{p}^{j+1}+\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{N+1}u_{a}\partial_{x_{\parallel}}v^{N+1}_{p}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\leq N-1\\
j\leq N\\
k\geq 0\\
i+j+k\geq N\end{subarray}}\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{i+j+k+1}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}\partial_{x_{3}}^{k+1}v_{e}^{j}|_{{x_{3}}=0}\cdot v_{p}^{i+1}+\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{N+1}v_{p}^{N+1}\sum_{j=0}^{N}\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{j}\partial_{x_{3}}v_{e}^{j}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k+j\geq N\\
j\leq N-1\\
k\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{k+j}}{k!}z^{k}\partial_{x_{3}}^{k}v_{e}^{0}|_{{x_{3}}=0}\cdot\partial_{z}v_{p}^{j+1}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j\geq N\\
0\leq i,j\leq N-1\end{subarray}}\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{i+j+1}(v_{e}^{i+1}|_{{x_{3}}=0}+v_{p}^{i+1})\partial_{z}v_{p}^{j+1}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j+k\geq N\\
i,j\leq N-1\\
k\geq 1\end{subarray}}\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{i+j+k+1}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}\partial_{x_{3}}^{k+1}v_{e}^{i+1}|_{{x_{3}}=0}\partial_{z}v_{p}^{j+1}+\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{N+1}v_{p}^{N+1}\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{j}\partial_{z}v_{p}^{j+1}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{N}\partial_{z}v_{p}^{N+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 1\\
\end{subarray}}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{k}\partial_{x_{3}}^{k}v_{e}^{0}|_{{x_{3}}=0}

\displaystyle+\partial_{z}v_{p}^{N+1}\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{N+i+1}\left(v_{e}^{i+1}+v_{p}^{i+1}\right)+\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{2N+1}v_{p}^{N+1}\partial_{z}v_{p}^{N+1}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{N+1}\partial_{t}v_{p}^{N+1}-\sum_{m=N+1}^{N+2}\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{m}(\Delta v_{e}^{m-2}+\Delta_{x_{\parallel}}v_{p}^{m-2}).

We define

\textbf{NS}(U)=\begin{bmatrix}\text{NS}_{\parallel}(u_{a})\\
\text{NS}_{x_{3}}(v_{a})\end{bmatrix}.

Theorem 2.2
.

There holds

\left\|\textbf{NS}(U)\right\|_{\tau_{0},2}+\left\|{\rm div}U\right\|_{\tau_{0},2}\lesssim{\kappa}^{\frac{N}{2}}.

Proof.

It suffices to bound the infinite sum terms appearing in
(
2.8
)
and
(
2.9
)
. We will give the proof for one term only, as all the other terms can be done similarly. The key idea is that the expansion is derived through the Taylor expansion of the Euler solutions near the boundary, in
x_{3}=\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}z
. In fact, to build the approximate solution, we only need to expand up to a finite, but large order, and then use the truncated Taylor expansion. The left over term can be estimated thanks to the Mean Value theorem for the Taylor expansion. To illustrate the method, we give the bound the analytic norm for the term

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq i,j\leq N\\
k\geq 0\\
i+j+k\geq N+1\end{subarray}}\frac{\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa}^{i+j+k}z^{k}}{k!}u_{p}^{i}\partial_{x_{3}}^{k}\partial_{x}u_{e}^{j}|_{{x_{3}}=0}

which is the second term in
(
2.8
)
. We fix
i,j\leq N
.
It suffices to consider
k\geq 2N+1
, since if
k\leq 2N
, the above sum is a sum of finite terms, and hence it is analytic in
\varepsilon\partial_{t}
and
\partial_{x_{\parallel}}
, using the analyticity of
u_{p}^{i}
and
u_{e}^{j}|_{x_{3}=0}
. Fix
i,j\in[0,N]
, we consider

\sum_{k=2N+1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}{(\eta_{0}\kappa)}^{\frac{i+j+k}{2}}z^{k}u_{p}^{i}\partial_{x_{3}}^{k}\nabla_{x_{\parallel}}\cdot u_{e}^{j}|_{x_{3}=0}.

Define
\widetilde{u}_{e}^{j}=\nabla_{x_{\parallel}}\cdot u_{e}^{j}
. The above term can be written as

\sum_{k=2N+1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}{(\eta_{0}\kappa)}^{\frac{i+j+k}{2}}z^{k}u_{p}^{i}\partial_{x_{3}}^{k}\widetilde{u}_{e}^{j}|_{x_{3}=0}=(\eta_{0}\kappa)^{\frac{i+j}{2}}u_{p}^{i}\sum_{k=2N+1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}x_{3}^{k}\partial_{x_{3}}^{k}\widetilde{u}_{e}^{j}|_{x_{3}=0}.

(2.10)

By the Mean Value theorem, there exists
x_{3}^{\star}\in(0,x_{3})
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=2N+1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}x_{3}^{k}\partial_{x_{3}}^{k}\widetilde{u}_{e}^{j}|_{x_{3}=0}\widetilde{u}_{e}^{j}

\displaystyle=x_{3}^{2N+1}\frac{1}{(2N+1)!}\partial_{x_{3}}^{2N+1}\widetilde{u}_{e}^{j}(x_{3}^{\star})

\displaystyle=(z\sqrt{\eta_{0}\kappa})^{2N+1}\frac{1}{(2N+1)!}\partial_{x_{3}}^{2N+1}\widetilde{u}_{e}^{j}(x_{3}^{\star}).

Hence, the term
(
2.10
)
can be written as

(\eta_{0}\kappa)^{\frac{i+j}{2}+N+\frac{1}{2}}z^{N+\frac{1}{2}}u_{p}^{i}\frac{1}{(2N+1)!}\partial_{x_{3}}^{2N+1}\widetilde{u}_{e}^{j}(x_{3}^{\star}).

Since the Euler solution is analytic with radius
\tau_{0}>0
, the above term can be bounded by

(\eta_{0}\kappa)^{\frac{i+j+1}{2}+N}\left\|z^{N+\frac{1}{2}}u_{p}^{i}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{z}}\tau_{0}^{-(2N+1)}\lesssim\kappa^{\frac{N}{2}}.

Here, we use also use the exponential decay of the Prandtl solutions, which are standard, see, for example
[
79
]
.
The proof is complete. ∎

2.2
Local Maxwellian of the Prandtl layers

In this section, we collect and prove estimates on the analytic norm of the local Maxwellian around a velocity
\varepsilon U(t,x)
where
x\in\Omega={\mathbb{T}}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
s_{0}>0
be a positive number. We can make
\tau_{0}\ll\tau_{U}
and
p_{0}\ll 1
such that

\sum_{|\alpha|\geq 1}\frac{\tau_{0}^{|\alpha|}}{\alpha!}\left\|e^{p_{0}(|\xi|^{2}+|\varphi|^{2})}\partial^{\alpha}(\mu^{s_{0}})\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,\xi}}\lesssim\varepsilon\kappa^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(2.11)

Proof.

First, we show
(
2.11
)
.
We note that

\mu^{s_{0}}(t,x,\xi)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3s_{0}/2}}e^{-s_{0}\frac{|\xi-\varepsilon U|^{2}}{2}}.

Up to scaling, we may assume
s_{0}=2
. Since a product of analytic functions is analytic, it suffices to justify that, for fixed
\ell\in\{1,2,3\}
,

\sum_{|\alpha|\geq 1}\frac{\tau_{0}^{|\alpha|}}{\alpha!}\left\{\left|\partial^{\alpha}\left(e^{-(\xi_{\ell}-\varepsilon U_{\ell})^{2}}\right)\right|+\left|e^{p_{0}(\xi_{\ell}-\varepsilon U_{\ell})^{2}}\partial^{\alpha}\left(e^{-(\xi_{\ell}-\varepsilon U_{\ell})^{2}}\right)\right|\right\}\lesssim\varepsilon\sqrt{\kappa}.

Combining Proposition
5.3
and Lemma
5.4
, we get the result. ∎

Lemma 2.4
.

Assume that

\sup_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3}}\frac{\tau_{0}^{|\alpha|}}{\alpha!}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{x_{3}}U\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim{\kappa}^{-\frac{1}{2}},\qquad\sup_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3}}\frac{\tau_{0}^{|\alpha|}}{\alpha!}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{x_{i}}U\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim 1\quad\text{for}\quad i\in\{1,2\}

and

\sup_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3}}\frac{\tau_{0}^{|\alpha|}}{\alpha!}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}(\varepsilon\partial_{t}U)\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim 1,

then for
\rho\ll p_{0}
, we have

\sum_{|\alpha|\geq 1}\frac{\tau_{0}^{|\alpha|}}{\alpha!}\left\|e^{\rho|\xi|^{2}}\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{x_{3}}\left(\mu^{p_{0}}\right)\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,\xi}}\lesssim\varepsilon\kappa^{-\frac{1}{2}},\qquad\sum_{|\alpha|\geq 1}\frac{\tau_{0}^{|\alpha|}}{\alpha!}\left\|e^{\rho|\xi|^{2}}\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{x_{i}}\left(\mu^{p_{0}}\right)\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,\xi}}\lesssim\varepsilon\quad\text{for}\quad i\in\{1,2\}

and

\sum_{|\alpha|\geq 1}\frac{\tau_{0}^{|\alpha|}}{\alpha!}\left\|e^{\rho|\xi|^{2}}\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{t}\left(\mu^{p_{0}}\right)\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,\xi}}\lesssim 1.

Proof.

We show the inequalities for
\partial_{x_{3}}
only. Up to scaling, we may assume
p_{0}=2
. We have

\partial_{x_{3}}\left(e^{-|\xi-\varepsilon U|^{2}}\right)=-2(\xi-\varepsilon U)\cdot\varepsilon\partial_{x_{3}}Ue^{-|\xi-\varepsilon U|^{2}}=2\varepsilon\partial_{x_{3}}U\cdot(\xi-\varepsilon U)e^{-|\xi-\varepsilon U|^{2}}

Hence

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3}}\frac{\tau_{0}^{|\alpha|}}{\alpha!}\left|\langle\varphi\rangle^{m}\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{x_{3}}\left(e^{-|\xi-\varepsilon U|^{2}}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3}}\frac{\tau_{0}^{|\alpha|}}{\alpha!}\left|\varepsilon\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{x_{3}}U\right|\cdot\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3}}\frac{\tau_{0}^{|\alpha|}}{\alpha!}\left|\langle\varphi\rangle^{m}\partial^{\alpha}\left(\varphi e^{-|\varphi|^{2}}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\kappa^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3}}\frac{\tau_{0}^{|\alpha|}}{\alpha!}\left|\partial^{\alpha}\left(\varphi e^{-|\varphi|^{2}}\right)\right|\lesssim 1.

using
(
2.11
)
. The proof is complete.
∎

3
Analysis of the Boltzmann equations

3.1
Preliminaries

In this paper, we recall

\Gamma(f,g)=\Gamma_{+}(f,g)-\Gamma_{-}(f,g)

(3.1)

where

\begin{cases}\Gamma_{+}(f,g)&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}|(\xi-\xi_{\star})\cdot{\omega}|\sqrt{\mu}(\xi_{\star})\left(f(\xi^{\prime})g(\xi_{\star}^{\prime})+g(\xi^{\prime})f(\xi_{\star}^{\prime})\right)d{\omega}d\xi_{\star},\\
\Gamma_{-}(f,g)&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}|(\xi-\xi_{\star})\cdot{\omega}|\sqrt{\mu}(\xi_{\star})\left(f(\xi)g(\xi_{\star})+g(\xi)f(\xi_{\star})\right)d{\omega}d\xi_{\star},\end{cases}

(3.2)

and

\begin{cases}\xi^{\prime}&=\xi-\left\{(\xi-\xi_{\star})\cdot{\omega}\right\}{\omega}\\
\xi_{\star}^{\prime}&=\xi_{\star}+\left\{(\xi-\xi_{\star})\cdot{\omega}\right\}{\omega}.\\
\end{cases}

(3.3)

We also recall the linear operator around the local Maxwellian
\mu(\xi)=\mu_{0}(\xi-\varepsilon U)
, given by

\displaystyle Lf

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}|(\xi-\xi_{\star})\cdot{\omega}|\sqrt{\mu}(\xi_{\star})

(3.4)

\displaystyle\times\left\{\sqrt{\mu}(\xi^{\prime})f(\xi_{\star}^{\prime})+f(\xi^{\prime})\sqrt{\mu}(\xi_{\star}^{\prime})-\sqrt{\mu}(\xi)f(\xi_{\star})-f(\xi)\sqrt{\mu}(\xi_{\star})\right\}d{\omega}d\xi_{\star}.

We also define

\displaystyle L_{0}f(\xi)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}|(\xi-\xi_{\star})\cdot{\omega}|\sqrt{\mu}_{0}(\xi_{\star})

(3.5)

\displaystyle\times\left\{\sqrt{\mu}_{0}(\xi^{\prime})f(\xi_{\star}^{\prime})+f(\xi^{\prime})\sqrt{\mu}_{0}(\xi_{\star}^{\prime})-\sqrt{\mu}_{0}(\xi)f(\xi_{\star})-f(\xi)\sqrt{\mu}_{0}(\xi_{\star})\right\}d{\omega}d\xi_{\star}.

For
i,j\in\{1,2,3\}
and
\varphi=\xi-\varepsilon U\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we define

\hat{A}_{ij}(\varphi)=\left(\varphi_{i}\varphi_{j}-\frac{1}{3}\delta_{ij}|\varphi|^{2}\right)\sqrt{\mu}_{0}(\varphi),\qquad A_{ij}(\varphi)=L^{-1}(\hat{A}_{ij}).

(3.6)

3.2
Real Analytic Function spaces

Recall
(
2.6
)
and
(
2.7
)
. For a function
f=f(t,x,\xi)
where
(t,x,\xi)\in[0,\infty)\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
and a function
\tau:t\in[0,\infty)\to\tau(t)\in[0,\infty)
, we define the following real-analytic norms:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{f}(t)

\displaystyle=\left\|\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(t)\left\|\partial^{\alpha}f(t)\right\|_{L^{2}_{x,\xi}}\right\|_{\ell_{\alpha}^{2}},

(3.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{f}(t)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-1}\kappa^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left\|\left\|\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(t)({\mathbf{I}}-\mathbf{P})\partial^{\alpha}f(t)\right\|_{L^{2}_{x,\xi}}\right\|_{\ell_{\alpha}^{2}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\rho,f}(t)

\displaystyle=\left\|\left\|\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(t)e^{\rho|\xi|^{2}}\partial^{\alpha}f(t)\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{t,x,\xi}}\right\|_{\ell_{\alpha}^{2}}.

To treat the loss of derivative, we also define the norm

\mathcal{Y}_{f}(t)^{2}=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3}}\frac{|\alpha|+1}{\tau}\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(t)^{2}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{x,\xi}}^{2}.

For a function
f(t,x,\xi)
defined on
t\geq 0
,
x\in\Omega
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we denote

\mathbf{P}f(t,x,\xi)=\left(a+b\cdot\varphi+c\frac{|\varphi|^{2}-3}{2}\right)\sqrt{\mu}.

(3.8)

For
1\leq p\leq\infty
, we denote

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{P}f\right\|_{L^{p}_{x}}

\displaystyle=\|a\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\|b_{i}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+\|c\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)},

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{P}f\right\|_{L^{p}_{x,\xi}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\int_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}|\mathbf{P}f(t,x,\xi)|^{p}dxd\xi\right\}^{1/p}.

We also denote

f_{\alpha}=\partial^{\alpha}f

a_{\alpha}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f_{\alpha}\sqrt{\mu}d\xi,\quad b_{\alpha}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f_{\alpha}\varphi\sqrt{\mu}d\xi,\qquad c_{\alpha}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f_{\alpha}\frac{|\varphi|^{2}-3}{3}\sqrt{\mu}d\xi.

From
(
3.8
)
, it is obvious that

\mathbf{P}f_{\alpha}=a_{\alpha}\sqrt{\mu}+b_{\alpha}\cdot\varphi\sqrt{\mu}+c_{\alpha}\frac{|\varphi|^{2}-3}{2}\sqrt{\mu}.

(3.9)

3.3
Remainder Equations

We consider the equation
(
1.1
)
.
For a general velocity field
U=U(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
where
t\geq 0
and
x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
and a scalar function
P
,
we define

\textbf{NS}(U,P)=\partial_{t}U+(U\cdot\nabla_{x})U+\nabla_{x}\left(P-\frac{\eta_{0}}{3}{\rm div}U\right)-\kappa\eta_{0}\Delta U.

(3.10)

We define

{\bf{f_{2}}}=P\sqrt{\mu}-\kappa\sum_{i,j}\partial_{i}U_{j}A_{ij}(\varphi).

(3.11)

For
L^{2}
estimate, we expand the Boltzmann solution
F
around
\mu
as follows

F=\mu+\varepsilon^{2}{\bf{f_{2}}}\sqrt{\mu}+\varepsilon\delta\sqrt{\mu}f,

(3.12)

For the
L^{\infty}
estimate, we will use the expansion

F=\mu+\sqrt{\mu}\varepsilon^{2}{\bf{f_{2}}}+\varepsilon\delta\sqrt{\mu}_{M}h,

(3.13)

where

\mu_{M}(\xi)=\frac{1}{(2\pi T_{M})^{\frac{3}{2}}}e^{-\frac{|\xi|^{2}}{2T_{M}}},\qquad\frac{1}{2}<T_{M}<1.

(3.14)

We define the error term

\textbf{R}_{a}=(\partial_{t}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})(\mu+\sqrt{\mu}\varepsilon^{2}{\bf{f_{2}}})-\frac{2}{\kappa}Q(\mu,{\bf{f_{2}}}\sqrt{\mu})-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\kappa}Q(\sqrt{\mu}{\bf{f_{2}}},\sqrt{\mu}{\bf{f_{2}}}),

(3.15)

the bilinear terms

\displaystyle\Gamma_{M}(f,g)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu_{M}}}Q(\sqrt{\mu_{M}}f,\sqrt{\mu_{M}}g)=\Gamma_{M,+}(f,g)-\Gamma_{M,-}(f,g),

(3.16)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{M,+}(f,g)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}|(\xi-\xi_{\star})\cdot{\omega}|\sqrt{\mu}_{M}(\xi_{\star})\left(f(\xi^{\prime})g(\xi_{\star}^{\prime})+g(\xi^{\prime})f(\xi_{\star}^{\prime})\right)d{\omega}d\xi_{\star},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{M,-}(f,g)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}|(\xi-\xi_{\star})\cdot{\omega}|\sqrt{\mu}_{M}(\xi_{\star})\left(f(\xi)g(\xi_{\star})+g(\xi)f(\xi_{\star})\right)d{\omega}d\xi_{\star},

and the linearized operator

\displaystyle L_{M}h

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{\sqrt{\mu_{M}}}Q(\mu,\sqrt{\mu_{M}}h).

We recall the following properties on
L_{M}
and
L
.

Lemma 3.1
.

For any
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, there exists a constant
C_{\varepsilon_{0}}>0
such that

L_{M}h=\nu_{M}(\xi)h-K_{M}h,

where

K_{M}h=K_{\varepsilon_{0}}h+K_{c}h,

and

\begin{cases}\nu_{M}(t,x,\xi)&\sim\nu_{0}(\xi)=\langle\xi\rangle,\\
K_{\varepsilon_{0}}h&\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}\nu_{0}(\xi)\left\|h\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{\xi}},\\
\ K_{c}h&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}k(\xi,\xi_{\star})h(\xi_{\star})d\xi_{\star}.\end{cases}

The kernel
k
satisfies the bound

\displaystyle k(\xi,\xi_{\star})\lesssim C_{\varepsilon_{0}}k_{\rho_{0}}(\xi,\xi_{\star}),\qquad k_{\rho_{0}}(\xi,\xi_{\star})=|\xi-\xi_{\star}|^{-1}e^{-\rho_{0}|\xi-\xi_{\star}|^{2}}

for some universal constant
\rho_{0}>0
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
f=f(t,x,\xi)
and
L
is defined in
(
3.4
)
. There holds

Lf=\nu f+K_{1}f-K_{2}f.

Here

\nu(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}|(\xi-\xi_{\star})\cdot{\omega}|\mu(\xi_{\star})d{\omega}d\xi_{\star}

(3.17)

The linear operator
K_{1}
is defined by

K_{1}f(\xi)=\int_{\xi_{\star}}\textbf{k}_{1}(\xi,\xi_{\star})f(\xi_{\star})d\xi_{\star},\qquad\textbf{k}_{1}(\xi,\xi_{\star})=\sqrt{\mu}(\xi)\sqrt{\mu}(\xi_{\star})\int_{|{\omega}|=1}|(\xi-\xi_{\star})\cdot{\omega}|d{\omega}.

(3.18)

The linear operator
K_{2}
is given by

K_{2}f=C_{0}\int_{V_{\parallel}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\textbf{k}_{2}(\zeta_{\perp},\zeta_{\parallel},V_{\parallel})f(\xi+V_{\parallel})dV_{\parallel},

where

\begin{cases}&\textbf{k}_{2}(\zeta_{\perp},\zeta_{\parallel},V_{\parallel})=\frac{1}{|V_{\parallel}|}e^{-\frac{1}{8}|V_{\parallel}|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}|\zeta_{\parallel}|^{2}}\int_{V_{\perp}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}|\zeta_{\perp}+V_{\perp}|^{2}}dV_{\perp},\\
&\zeta_{\parallel}=\frac{(\xi-\varepsilon U)\cdot V_{\parallel}}{|V_{\parallel}|^{2}}V_{\parallel}+\frac{1}{2}V_{\parallel},\\
&\zeta_{\perp}=\xi-\varepsilon U-\frac{(\xi-\varepsilon U)\cdot V_{\parallel}}{\|V_{\parallel}\|^{2}}V_{\parallel},\end{cases}

(3.19)

and
C_{0}
is a universal constant. Moreover, one has

\left\|e^{\rho|\xi|^{2}}L^{-1}g\right\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}}\lesssim\left\|e^{\rho|\xi|^{2}}\nu^{-1}g\right\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}}.

(3.20)

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle Lf

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\mu}(\xi)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}|(\xi-\xi_{\star})\cdot{\omega}|\sqrt{\mu}(\xi_{\star})f(\xi_{\star})d{\omega}d\xi_{\star}+f(\xi)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}|(\xi-\xi_{\star})\cdot{\omega}|\mu(\xi_{\star})d{\omega}d\xi_{\star}

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}|(\xi-\xi_{\star})\cdot{\omega}|\sqrt{\mu}(\xi_{\star})\left\{\sqrt{\mu}(\xi^{\prime})f(\xi_{\star}^{\prime})+f(\xi^{\prime})\sqrt{\mu}(\xi_{\star}^{\prime})\right\}d{\omega}d\xi_{\star}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\textbf{k}_{1}(\xi,\xi_{\star})f(\xi_{\star})d\xi_{\star}+\nu(\xi)f(\xi)-K_{2}f,

where

\displaystyle K_{2}f

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}|(\xi-\xi_{\star})\cdot{\omega}|\sqrt{\mu}(\xi_{\star})\left\{\sqrt{\mu}(\xi^{\prime})f(\xi_{\star}^{\prime})+f(\xi^{\prime})\sqrt{\mu}(\xi_{\star}^{\prime})\right\}d{\omega}d\xi_{\star}.

Let

V=\xi_{\star}-\xi.

Then we have

\displaystyle\xi_{\star}

\displaystyle=\xi+V,

\displaystyle\xi_{\star}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\xi_{\star}-({\omega}\cdot(\xi_{\star}-\xi)){\omega}=V+\xi-({\omega}\cdot V){\omega}=\xi+V_{\perp},

\displaystyle\xi^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\xi+((\xi_{\star}-\xi)\cdot{\omega}){\omega}=\xi+V_{\parallel}.

where
V_{\parallel}
is the vector projection of
V
onto
{\omega}
and
V_{\perp}=V-V_{\parallel}
.
Thus we get

\displaystyle K_{2}f

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}|V_{\parallel}|\sqrt{\mu}(\xi+V)\sqrt{\mu}(\xi+V_{\perp})f(\xi+V_{\parallel})d{\omega}dV

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}|V_{\parallel}|\sqrt{\mu}(\xi+V)\sqrt{\mu}(\xi+V_{\parallel})f(\xi+V_{\perp})d{\omega}dV

\displaystyle=K_{2,1}f+K_{2,2}f.

We first simplify
K_{2,1}
. We will integrate in
V_{\perp}
first, then
V_{\parallel}
and then
{\omega}
. For fixed
{\omega}\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
, we have

V_{\perp}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\qquad V_{\parallel}\in\mathbb{R}.

Namely,
V_{\perp}
lies on the plane that is perpendicular to
{\omega}
and
V_{\parallel}
lies on the axis containing
{\omega}
.
Now we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}dV=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dV_{\parallel}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}dV_{\perp}=2\int_{0}^{\infty}dV_{\parallel}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}dV_{\perp}.

Hence

d{\omega}dV=d{\omega}\cdot 2dV_{\perp}d|V_{\parallel}|=2dV_{\perp}\frac{|V_{\parallel}|^{2}d|V_{\parallel}|d{\omega}}{|V_{\parallel}|^{2}}=\frac{2}{|V_{\parallel}|^{2}}dV_{\perp}dV_{\parallel},\qquad V_{\parallel}\in\mathbb{R}^{3},\quad V_{\perp}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

This implies

\displaystyle K_{2,1}f

\displaystyle=2\int_{V_{\perp}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{V_{\parallel}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}|V_{\parallel}|^{-1}\sqrt{\mu}(\xi+V)\sqrt{\mu}(\xi+V_{\perp})f(\xi+V_{\parallel})dV_{\parallel}dV_{\perp}

\displaystyle=2\int_{V_{\perp}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{V_{\parallel}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}|V_{\parallel}|^{-1}\mu_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\xi-\varepsilon U+V)\mu_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\xi-\varepsilon U+V_{\perp})f(\xi+V_{\parallel})dV_{\parallel}dV_{\perp}.

Let
\zeta=\xi-\varepsilon U+\frac{1}{2}V_{\parallel}
. Define
(\zeta_{\parallel},\zeta_{\perp})
so that

\zeta=\zeta_{\parallel}+\zeta_{\perp},\quad\zeta_{\parallel}\parallel V_{\parallel},\quad\zeta_{\perp}\parallel V_{\perp}.

We have the explicit formula

\begin{cases}\zeta_{\parallel}(t,x,\xi)&=\frac{(\xi-\varepsilon U+\frac{1}{2}V_{\parallel})\cdot V_{\parallel}}{|V_{\parallel}|^{2}}V_{\parallel}=\frac{(\xi-\varepsilon U)\cdot V_{\parallel}}{|V_{\parallel}|^{2}}V_{\parallel}+\frac{1}{2}V_{\parallel},\\
\zeta_{\perp}(t,x,\xi)&=(\xi-\varepsilon U+\frac{1}{2}V_{\parallel})-\zeta_{\parallel}=\xi-\varepsilon U-\frac{(\xi-\varepsilon U)\cdot V_{\parallel}}{|V_{\parallel}|^{2}}V_{\parallel}.\end{cases}

Now we have

\begin{cases}\xi-\varepsilon U+V&=V_{\perp}+\zeta_{\perp}+\left(\zeta_{\parallel}+\frac{1}{2}V_{\parallel}\right),\\
\xi-\varepsilon U+V_{\perp}&=V_{\perp}+\zeta_{\perp}+\left(\zeta_{\parallel}-\frac{1}{2}V_{\parallel}\right).\end{cases}

Hence

\displaystyle\mu_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\xi-\varepsilon U+V)\mu_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\xi-\varepsilon U+V_{\perp})

\displaystyle=C_{0}\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2}\left|V_{\perp}+\zeta_{\perp}\right|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}|\zeta_{\parallel}|^{2}-\frac{1}{8}|V_{\parallel}|^{2}\right\}.

Hence we have

K_{2,1}f=C_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{1}{|V_{\parallel}|}\left\{\int_{V_{\perp}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}|V_{\perp}+\zeta_{\perp}|^{2}}dV_{\perp}\right\}e^{-\frac{1}{2}|\zeta_{\parallel}|^{2}-\frac{1}{8}|V_{\parallel}|^{2}}f(\xi+V_{\parallel})dV_{\parallel}.

∎

Definition 3.3
.

We define

\displaystyle\gamma_{-}

\displaystyle=\{x\in\partial\Omega,\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:\quad n(x)\cdot\xi<0\},

\displaystyle\gamma_{+}

\displaystyle=\{x\in\partial\Omega,\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:\quad n(x)\cdot\xi>0\}.

When
\Omega={\mathbb{T}}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we have

\displaystyle\gamma_{-}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{T}}^{2}\times\{x_{3}=0\}\times\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:\xi_{3}>0\},

\displaystyle\gamma_{+}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{T}}^{2}\times\{x_{3}=0\}\times\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:\xi_{3}<0\}.

We define the boundary integration for a function
f(t,x,\xi)
where
x\in\partial\Omega
as follows

\int_{\gamma_{\pm}}fd\gamma=\int_{\gamma_{\pm}}f|n(x)\cdot\xi|dS(x)d\xi,

where
dS(x)
denotes the standard surface measure on
\partial\Omega
. We also define

\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p}_{\gamma}}=\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p}_{\gamma_{+}}}+\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p}_{\gamma_{-}}}

to be the
L^{p}(\gamma)
with respect to the measure
|n(x)\cdot\xi|dS(x)d\xi
.
We also define the boundary inner product over
\gamma_{\pm}
in
\xi
as

\langle f,g\rangle_{\gamma_{\pm}}(t,x)=\int_{\pm n(x)\cdot\xi>0}f(t,x,\xi)g(t,x,\xi)|n(x)\cdot\xi|d\xi.

The projection operator
\mathbf{P}_{\gamma_{+}}
onto the unit vector
\sqrt{c_{\mu}\mu_{0}(\xi)}
with respect to
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\gamma_{+}}
is defined as follows

(\mathbf{P}_{\gamma_{+}}f)(x,\xi)=c_{\mu}\sqrt{\mu_{0}(\xi)}\left\{\int_{(x,\widetilde{\xi})\in\gamma_{+}}f(x,\widetilde{\xi})\sqrt{\mu_{0}(\widetilde{\xi})}|n(x)\cdot\widetilde{\xi}|d\widetilde{\xi}\right\}.

(3.21)

When
\Omega={\mathbb{T}}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
, this can be written as

(\mathbf{P}_{\gamma_{+}}f)(t,x,\xi)=c_{\mu}\sqrt{\mu_{0}(\xi)}\left\{\int_{\widetilde{\xi}_{3}<0}f(t,x,\widetilde{\xi})\sqrt{\mu_{0}(\widetilde{\xi})}|\widetilde{\xi}_{3}|d\widetilde{\xi}\right\}.

(3.22)

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
F
solves
(
1.1
)
and
f,h
defined in the expansion
(
3.12
)
and
(
3.13
)
. Then
f
and
h
solve

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x}f+\frac{1}{\kappa\varepsilon^{2}}Lf+\frac{(\partial_{t}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})\sqrt{\mu}}{\sqrt{\mu}}f

(3.23)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\varepsilon\delta\sqrt{\mu}}\textbf{R}_{a}+\frac{\delta}{\kappa\varepsilon}\Gamma(f,f)+\frac{2}{\kappa}\Gamma({\bf{f_{2}}},f).

with the boundary condition

f|_{\gamma_{-}}=\mathbf{P}_{\gamma_{+}}f-\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}r,

(3.24)

where

r=(1-\mathbf{P}_{\gamma_{+}}){\bf{f_{2}}},

(3.25)

and

\partial_{t}h+\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x}h+\frac{1}{\kappa{\varepsilon}^{2}}L_{M}h=-\frac{1}{\varepsilon\delta\sqrt{\mu}_{M}}\textbf{R}_{a}+\frac{\delta}{\kappa\varepsilon}\Gamma_{M}(h,h)+\frac{2}{\kappa}\frac{Q({\bf{f_{2}}}\sqrt{\mu},h\sqrt{\mu_{M}})}{\sqrt{\mu_{M}}}.

(3.26)

Proof.

We show
(
3.23
)
, as the proof of
(
3.26
)
is similar.
Plugging
F=\mu+\varepsilon^{2}{\bf{f_{2}}}\sqrt{\mu}+\varepsilon\delta f\sqrt{\mu}
into the equation
(
1.1
)
, we get

\displaystyle\varepsilon\delta\sqrt{\mu}\left(\partial_{t}f+\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x}f+\frac{(\partial_{t}+\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})\sqrt{\mu}}{\sqrt{\mu}}f\right)+(\partial_{t}+\varepsilon^{-1}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})(\mu+{\varepsilon}^{2}{\bf{f_{2}}}\sqrt{\mu})

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\kappa}Q(\sqrt{\mu}{\bf{f_{2}}},\mu)+\frac{{\varepsilon}^{2}}{\kappa}Q(\sqrt{\mu}{\bf{f_{2}}},\sqrt{\mu}{\bf{f_{2}}})+\frac{2\delta}{\kappa\varepsilon}Q(\mu,\sqrt{\mu}f)

\displaystyle+\frac{2\delta{\varepsilon}}{\kappa}Q(\sqrt{\mu}{\bf{f_{2}}},\sqrt{\mu}f)+\frac{\delta^{2}}{\kappa}Q(\sqrt{\mu}f,\sqrt{\mu}f).

Hence we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f+\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x}f+\frac{(\partial_{t}+\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})\sqrt{\mu}}{\sqrt{\mu}}f+\frac{1}{\kappa{\varepsilon}^{2}}Lf

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\varepsilon\delta\sqrt{\mu}}\left\{(\partial_{t}+\varepsilon^{-1}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})(\mu+{\varepsilon}^{2}{\bf{f_{2}}}\sqrt{\mu})-\frac{2}{\kappa}Q(\sqrt{\mu}{\bf{f_{2}}},\mu)-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\kappa}Q(\sqrt{\mu}{\bf{f_{2}}},\sqrt{\mu}{\bf{f_{2}}})\right\}

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{\kappa}\Gamma({\bf{f_{2}}},f)+\frac{\delta}{\kappa{\varepsilon}}\Gamma(f,f).

The proof is complete.
∎

Proposition 3.5
.

Recalling
(
3.15
)
, we have

\displaystyle\textbf{R}_{a}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\textbf{NS}(U,P)\cdot\varphi\mu-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\kappa}\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\Gamma({\bf{f_{2}}},{\bf{f_{2}}})+\frac{1}{3}{\rm div}U|\varphi|^{2}\mu-\varepsilon\kappa\partial_{i\ell}U_{j}({\mathbf{I}}-\mathbf{P})(\varphi_{\ell}A_{ij})\sqrt{\mu}

(3.27)

\displaystyle+\varepsilon^{2}\mu\left\{(\partial_{t}P+U\cdot\nabla_{x}P)+\varepsilon P(\partial_{t}U+U\cdot\nabla_{x}U)\cdot\varphi+\frac{1}{2}P\partial_{i}U_{\ell}\varphi_{i}\varphi_{\ell}\right\}

\displaystyle-\varepsilon^{2}\kappa\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\{(\partial_{t}+U\cdot\nabla_{x})(\partial_{i}U_{j})+\varepsilon(\partial_{t}U+U\cdot\nabla_{x}U)\cdot\varphi\partial_{i}U_{j}+\partial_{i}U_{j}\partial_{k}U_{\ell}\varphi_{k}\varphi_{\ell}\right\}A_{ij}

\displaystyle-\varepsilon^{2}\kappa\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{i}U_{j}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{t}+(\varepsilon^{2}U+\varphi)\cdot\nabla_{x}\right)A_{ij}.

Proof.

We have

\textbf{R}_{a}=\left(\partial_{t}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x}\right)\mu+\varepsilon^{2}\left(\partial_{t}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x}\right)(\sqrt{\mu}{\bf{f_{2}}})-\frac{2}{\kappa}Q(\mu,{\bf{f_{2}}}\sqrt{\mu})-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\kappa}Q(\sqrt{\mu}{\bf{f_{2}}},\sqrt{\mu}{\bf{f_{2}}}).

First, we have

\displaystyle\frac{(\partial_{t}+\varepsilon^{-1}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})\mu}{\sqrt{\mu}}=\frac{(\partial_{t}+\varepsilon^{-1}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{x})\mu}{\sqrt{\mu}}+\frac{(\partial_{t}+U\cdot\nabla_{x})\mu}{\sqrt{\mu}}

(3.28)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(\partial_{t}U+U\cdot\nabla_{x}U)\cdot\varphi\sqrt{\mu}+\sum_{i,j}\partial_{i}U_{j}\varphi_{i}\varphi_{j}\sqrt{\mu}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(\partial_{t}U+U\cdot\nabla_{x}U)\cdot\varphi\sqrt{\mu}+\partial_{i}U_{j}\hat{A}_{ij}(\varphi)+\frac{1}{3}{\rm div}U(|\varphi|^{2}-3)\sqrt{\mu}+({\rm div}U)\sqrt{\mu}.

Since
\frac{1}{\kappa}L{\bf{f_{2}}}+\partial_{i}U_{j}\hat{A}_{ij}(\varphi)=0
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\kappa}Q(\mu,{\bf{f_{2}}}\sqrt{\mu})-(\partial_{t}+{\varepsilon}^{-1}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})\mu

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon(\partial_{t}U+U\cdot\nabla_{x}U)\cdot\varphi\mu-\frac{1}{3}(\nabla_{x}\cdot U)(|\varphi|^{2}-3)\mu-(\nabla_{x}\cdot U)\mu.

Hence

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})(\mu+\sqrt{\mu}\varepsilon^{2}{\bf{f_{2}}})-\frac{2}{\kappa}Q(\mu,{\bf{f_{2}}}\sqrt{\mu})-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\kappa}Q(\sqrt{\mu}{\bf{f_{2}}},\sqrt{\mu}{\bf{f_{2}}})

\displaystyle={\varepsilon}^{2}\sqrt{\mu}\left\{\partial_{t}{\bf{f_{2}}}+U\cdot\nabla_{x}{\bf{f_{2}}}+\frac{(\partial_{t}+\varepsilon^{-1}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})\sqrt{\mu}}{\sqrt{\mu}}{\bf{f_{2}}}\right\}

\displaystyle+\varepsilon\left\{\nabla_{x}\mathbf{P}{\bf{f_{2}}}+(\partial_{t}U+U\cdot\nabla_{x}U)\right\}\cdot\varphi\mu-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\kappa}\sqrt{\mu}\Gamma({\bf{f_{2}}},{\bf{f_{2}}})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{3}{\rm div}U|\varphi|^{2}\mu+\varepsilon\varphi\cdot\nabla_{x}({\mathbf{I}}-\mathbf{P}){\bf{f_{2}}}\sqrt{\mu}.

Now we simplify the last term in the above. Now we have

\displaystyle\varphi\cdot\nabla_{x}({\mathbf{I}}-\mathbf{P}){\bf{f_{2}}}

(3.29)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{k}\partial_{k}\left\{-\kappa\partial_{i}U_{j}A_{ij}(\varphi)\right\}

\displaystyle=-\kappa\varphi_{k}\partial_{ik}U_{j}A_{ij}(\varphi)-\kappa\partial_{i}U_{j}(\varphi\cdot\nabla_{x})A_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\kappa\mathbf{P}(\partial_{ik}U_{j}\varphi_{k}A_{ij})-\kappa\partial_{ik}U_{j}({\mathbf{I}}-\mathbf{P})(\varphi_{k}A_{ij})-\kappa\partial_{i}U_{j}(\varphi\cdot\nabla_{x})A_{ij}.

The first term is computed as follows

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(\partial_{ik}U_{j}\varphi_{k}A_{ij})

\displaystyle=\sum_{m}\partial_{ik}U_{j}\langle\varphi_{k}\varphi_{m}\sqrt{\mu},A_{ij}\rangle_{L^{2}_{\varphi}}\varphi_{m}\sqrt{\mu}=\partial_{ik}U_{j}\langle\hat{A}_{km},A_{ij}\rangle\varphi_{m}\sqrt{\mu}

(3.30)

\displaystyle=\eta_{0}\partial_{ik}U_{j}\left(\delta_{ki}\delta_{mj}+\delta_{kj}\delta_{mi}-\frac{2}{3}\delta_{km}\delta_{ij}\right)\varphi_{m}\sqrt{\mu}

\displaystyle=\eta_{0}(\Delta U_{m}+\frac{1}{3}\partial_{m}({\rm div}U))\varphi_{m}\sqrt{\mu}.

Hence

\displaystyle\varphi\cdot\nabla_{x}({\mathbf{I}}-\mathbf{P}){\bf{f_{2}}}

\displaystyle=-\kappa\eta_{0}\Delta U\cdot\varphi\sqrt{\mu}-\frac{1}{3}\kappa\eta_{0}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{x}({\rm div}U)\sqrt{\mu}-\kappa\partial_{ik}U_{j}({\mathbf{I}}-\mathbf{P})(\varphi_{k}A_{ij})-\kappa\partial_{i}U_{j}(\varphi\cdot\nabla_{x})A_{ij}.

Hence we obtain

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})(\mu+\sqrt{\mu}\varepsilon^{2}{\bf{f_{2}}})-\frac{2}{\kappa}Q(\mu,{\bf{f_{2}}}\sqrt{\mu})-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\kappa}Q(\sqrt{\mu}{\bf{f_{2}}},\sqrt{\mu}{\bf{f_{2}}})

\displaystyle={\varepsilon}^{2}\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\{\partial_{t}{\bf{f_{2}}}+U\cdot\nabla_{x}{\bf{f_{2}}}+\frac{(\partial_{t}+\varepsilon^{-1}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})\sqrt{\mu}}{\sqrt{\mu}}{\bf{f_{2}}}\right\}+\varepsilon\textbf{NS}(U,P)\cdot\varphi\mu

\displaystyle-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\kappa}\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\Gamma({\bf{f_{2}}},{\bf{f_{2}}})+\frac{1}{3}{\rm div}U|\varphi|^{2}\mu-\varepsilon\kappa\partial_{ik}U_{j}({\mathbf{I}}-\mathbf{P})(\varphi_{k}A_{ij})\sqrt{\mu}-\kappa\partial_{i}U_{j}(\varphi\cdot\nabla_{x})A_{ij}\sqrt{\mu}.

Lastly, we see that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}{\bf{f_{2}}}+U\cdot\nabla_{x}{\bf{f_{2}}}+\frac{(\partial_{t}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})\sqrt{\mu}}{\sqrt{\mu}}{\bf{f_{2}}}

\displaystyle=\left\{(\partial_{t}P+U\cdot\nabla_{x}P)+\varepsilon P(\partial_{t}U+U\cdot\nabla_{x}U)\cdot\varphi+\frac{1}{2}P\partial_{i}U_{k}\varphi_{i}\varphi_{k}\right\}\sqrt{\mu}

\displaystyle+A_{ij}(\varphi)\left\{-\kappa(\partial_{t}+U\cdot\nabla_{x})(\partial_{i}U_{j})-\varepsilon\kappa(\partial_{t}U+U\cdot\nabla_{x}U)\cdot\varphi\partial_{i}U_{j}-\kappa\partial_{i}U_{j}\partial_{k}U_{l}\varphi_{k}\varphi_{l}\right\}

\displaystyle-\kappa\partial_{i}U_{j}(\partial_{t}+U\cdot\nabla_{x})A_{ij}(\varphi).

The proof is complete.
∎

3.4
Equations and boundary conditions for derivatives

Fix
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3}
. Now we define

f_{\alpha}=\partial^{\alpha}f,\qquad h_{\alpha}=\partial^{\alpha}h

(3.31)

Applying
\partial^{\alpha}
to the equation
(
3.23
)
and let
f_{\alpha}=\partial^{\alpha}f
, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f_{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x}f_{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\kappa\varepsilon^{2}}Lf_{\alpha}+\frac{(\partial_{t}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})\sqrt{\mu}}{\sqrt{\mu}}f_{\alpha}

(3.32)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\kappa\varepsilon^{2}}[L,\partial^{\alpha}]f+g_{\alpha}+\frac{(\partial_{t}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})\sqrt{\mu}}{\sqrt{\mu}}f_{\alpha}-\partial^{\alpha}\left\{\frac{(\partial_{t}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})\sqrt{\mu}}{\sqrt{\mu}}f\right\}.

where

g_{\alpha}=\partial^{\alpha}\left(-\frac{1}{\varepsilon\delta\sqrt{\mu}}\textbf{R}_{a}+\frac{\delta}{\kappa\varepsilon}\Gamma(f,f)+\frac{2}{\kappa}\Gamma({\bf{f_{2}}},f)\right),

(3.33)

along with the boundary condition

f_{\alpha}|_{\gamma_{-}}=\mathbf{P}_{\gamma}f_{\alpha}-\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}\partial^{\alpha}r.

(3.34)

where
r
is given in
(
3.25
)
.
Next, it is clear from
(
3.26
)
that
h_{\alpha}
and Lemma
3.1
that
h_{\alpha}=\partial^{\alpha}h
solves the equation

\partial_{t}h_{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x}h_{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\kappa{\varepsilon}^{2}}L_{M}h_{\alpha}=\widetilde{g}_{\alpha}

(3.35)

where

\widetilde{g}_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{\kappa\varepsilon^{2}}[L_{M},\partial^{\alpha}]h+\partial^{\alpha}\left(-\frac{1}{\varepsilon\delta\sqrt{\mu}_{M}}\textbf{R}_{a}+\frac{\delta}{\kappa{\varepsilon}}\Gamma_{M}(h,h)+\frac{2}{\kappa}\frac{Q({\bf{f_{2}}}\sqrt{\mu},h\sqrt{\mu_{M}})}{\sqrt{\mu_{M}}}\right).

Recalling the boundary condition for
f
in
(
3.24
)
and the fact that
f=\frac{\sqrt{\mu_{M}}}{\sqrt{\mu}_{0}}h
on the boundary and the calculation
(
3.57
)
, we get

h_{\alpha}|_{\{x_{3}=0,\xi_{3}>0\}}=c_{\mu}w_{1}(\xi)\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}h_{\alpha}(t,x,\xi_{\star})w_{2}(\xi_{\star})d\xi_{\star}\right\}-\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}w_{3}(\xi)^{-1}\partial^{\alpha}r

(3.36)

where

w_{1}(\xi)=e^{-\frac{|\xi|^{2}}{4}(2-T_{M}^{-1})},\qquad w_{2}(\xi)=1_{\{\xi_{3}<0\}}e^{-\frac{|\xi|^{2}}{4T_{M}}}|\xi_{3}|,\qquad w_{3}(\xi)=T_{M}^{\frac{3}{2}}e^{-\frac{|\xi|^{2}}{4}(T_{M}^{-1}-1)}.

We note that
w_{1},w_{2}
and
w_{3}
are decaying functions in
\xi
.

We now derive the boundary condition for
b
, where we recall
(
3.8
)
. We recall that

F=\mu+\varepsilon^{2}{\bf{f_{2}}}\sqrt{\mu}+\varepsilon\delta f\sqrt{\mu}.

Proposition 3.6
.

Assume
(
3.8
)
. Then we have

b_{3}|_{\partial\Omega}=0.

(3.37)

Moreover, recalling
(
3.9
)
, we have

b_{\alpha,3}|_{x_{3}=0}=0.

Here
\partial^{\alpha}=(\varepsilon\partial_{t})^{\alpha_{0}}\partial_{x_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\partial_{x_{2}}^{\alpha_{2}}
for
\alpha=(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{3}
.

Proof.

First we will show that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\partial^{\alpha}F\xi_{3}d\xi=0
. Let
z_{\gamma}(x)=\int_{\xi_{3}<0}F|\xi_{3}|d\xi_{3}
, then we see that

\partial^{\alpha}z_{\gamma}=\int_{\xi_{3}<0}\partial^{\alpha}F|\xi_{3}|d\xi_{3}.

The boundary condition implies

\partial^{\alpha}F|_{x_{3}=0,\xi_{3}>0}=c_{\mu}\mu_{0}(\xi)\partial^{\alpha}z_{\gamma}(x).

We have

\displaystyle\int_{\xi_{3}}\partial^{\alpha}F\xi_{3}d\xi_{3}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\partial^{\alpha}F\xi_{3}d\xi_{3}+\int_{0}^{\infty}\partial^{\alpha}F\xi_{3}d\xi_{3}

\displaystyle=-\int_{-\infty}^{0}\partial^{\alpha}F|\xi_{3}|d\xi_{3}+\int_{0}^{\infty}c_{\mu}\mu_{0}(\xi)\partial^{\alpha}z_{\gamma}(x)\xi_{3}d\xi.

Hence

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\partial^{\alpha}F\xi_{3}d\xi_{3}

\displaystyle=-\partial^{\alpha}z_{\gamma}(x)+\partial^{\alpha}z_{\gamma}(x)c_{\mu}\int_{\xi_{3}>0}\mu_{0}(\xi)\xi_{3}d\xi=0.

Here we use the fact that

c_{\mu}\int_{\xi_{3}>0}\mu_{0}(\xi)|\xi_{3}|d\xi=1.

(3.38)

Now combining
F=\mu+\varepsilon\delta f\sqrt{\mu}+\varepsilon^{2}{\bf{f_{2}}}\sqrt{\mu}
and the identity
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\partial^{\alpha}F|_{x_{3}=0}\xi_{3}d\xi_{3}=0
, we get
\partial^{\alpha}b_{3}|_{x_{3}=0}=0
. The proof is complete.
∎

3.5
Conservation Laws

To begin, we first show the conservation law for
f
.

Proposition 3.7
.

Recalling
(
3.9
)
, we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon\partial_{t}a_{\alpha}+\nabla_{x}\cdot b_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon\partial_{j}(U_{j}a_{\alpha})-\frac{1}{\varepsilon\delta}\partial^{\alpha}(\nabla_{x}\cdot U)

(3.39)

\displaystyle-\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}\partial^{\alpha}(U_{i}\partial_{i}U_{i})-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\delta}\partial^{\alpha}(\partial_{t}P+\partial_{x_{j}}(U_{j}P))+\delta^{-1}\kappa\varepsilon^{2}c_{ij}\partial_{i}U_{j}(\nabla_{x}\cdot U)

\displaystyle-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta+\gamma=\alpha\\
|\gamma|\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{\alpha!}{\beta!\gamma!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(\varepsilon\partial_{t}+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})f_{\beta}\cdot\partial^{\gamma}\sqrt{\mu}d\xi

\displaystyle-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta+\gamma=\alpha\\
|\gamma|\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{\alpha!}{\beta!\gamma!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f_{\beta}(\varepsilon\partial_{t}+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})(\partial^{\gamma}\sqrt{\mu})d\xi.

where
c_{ij}
is defined in
(
3.42
)
.

Proof.

We use the equation
(
1.1
)
and the expansion
(
3.12
)
. Hence we get
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(\varepsilon\partial_{t}F+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x}F)d\xi=0
.
We have

\displaystyle\varepsilon\frac{d}{dt}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f\sqrt{\mu}d\xi\right)+\sum_{j}\partial_{x_{j}}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\xi_{j}f\sqrt{\mu}\right\}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\varepsilon\delta}({\varepsilon}\partial_{t}+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})\mu d\xi-\frac{{\varepsilon}}{\delta}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(\varepsilon\partial_{t}+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})({\bf{f_{2}}}\sqrt{\mu})d\xi.

This implies

\varepsilon\partial_{t}a+\nabla_{x}\cdot b+\varepsilon\partial_{j}(U_{j}a)=-\frac{1}{\varepsilon\delta}({\varepsilon}\partial_{t}+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})\mu d\xi-\frac{{\varepsilon}}{\delta}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(\varepsilon\partial_{t}+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})({\bf{f_{2}}}\sqrt{\mu})d\xi.

Next we compute the two terms on the right hand side of the above. We have, for any
\partial\in\{\partial_{x_{j}},\varepsilon\partial_{t}\}
,

\partial\mu=-(\xi-\varepsilon U)\cdot(-1)\varepsilon\partial U\mu=\varepsilon\partial U\cdot\varphi\mu.

Hence

\varepsilon\partial_{t}\mu+\xi_{i}\partial_{x_{i}}\mu=\varepsilon(\varepsilon\partial_{t}U\cdot\varphi)\mu+\xi_{i}\varepsilon\partial_{x_{i}}U_{j}\varphi_{j}\mu=\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{t}U\cdot\varphi\mu+\varphi_{i}\varphi_{j}\partial_{i}U_{j}\varepsilon\mu+\varepsilon^{2}U_{i}\partial_{i}U_{j}\varphi_{i}\varphi_{j}\mu.

And thus

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left(\varepsilon\partial_{t}\mu+\xi_{i}\partial_{x_{i}}\mu\right)d\xi=(\nabla_{x}\cdot U)+\varepsilon^{2}\sum_{i}U_{i}\partial_{i}U_{i}.

(3.40)

Next, we compute
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(\varepsilon\partial_{t}+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})({\bf{f_{2}}}\sqrt{\mu})d\xi
. Recalling
(
3.11
)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(\varepsilon\partial_{t}+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})({\bf{f_{2}}}\sqrt{\mu})d\xi

(3.41)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\partial_{t}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}P\mu d\xi\right\}+\partial_{x_{j}}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\xi_{j}(P\mu)\right\}-\kappa\partial_{i}U_{j}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(\varepsilon\partial_{t}+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})A_{ij}(\varphi)d\xi

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\partial_{t}P+\partial_{x_{j}}(\varepsilon U_{j}P)-\kappa\varepsilon c_{ij}\partial_{i}U_{j}(\nabla_{x}\cdot U).

where

c_{ij}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}A_{ij}(\varphi)d\varphi.

(3.42)

Now we show
(
3.39
)
. We have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\xi\partial^{\alpha}Fd\xi

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\delta(b_{\alpha}+\varepsilon Ua_{\alpha})+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\xi\left\{\partial^{\alpha}\mu+\varepsilon^{2}\partial^{\alpha}({\bf{f_{2}}}\sqrt{\mu})+\varepsilon\delta\sum_{\beta<\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}f_{\beta}\partial^{\alpha-\beta}\sqrt{\mu}\right\}d\xi,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\partial^{\alpha}Fd\xi

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\delta a_{\alpha}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left\{\partial^{\alpha}\mu+\varepsilon^{2}\partial^{\alpha}({\bf{f_{2}}}\sqrt{\mu})+\varepsilon\delta\sum_{\beta<\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}f_{\beta}\partial^{\alpha-\beta}\sqrt{\mu}\right\}d\xi.

Combining the above identities, we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon\partial_{t}a_{\alpha}+\nabla_{x}\cdot b_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon\partial_{j}(U_{j}a_{\alpha})-\frac{1}{\varepsilon\delta}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(\varepsilon\partial_{t}+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})(\partial^{\alpha}\mu)d\xi-\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(\varepsilon\partial_{t}+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})(\partial^{\alpha}({\bf{f_{2}}}\sqrt{\mu}))

\displaystyle-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta+\gamma=\alpha\\
|\gamma|\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{\alpha!}{\beta!\gamma!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(\varepsilon\partial_{t}+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})f_{\beta}\cdot\partial^{\gamma}\sqrt{\mu}d\xi

\displaystyle-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta+\gamma=\alpha\\
|\gamma|\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{\alpha!}{\beta!\gamma!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f_{\beta}(\varepsilon\partial_{t}+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})(\partial^{\gamma}\sqrt{\mu})d\xi.

Combining the above with
(
3.40
)
and
(
3.41
)
, we get the result.∎

3.6
L^{2}
estimate for
f
in analytic spaces

Our goal is to derive analytic energy estimates for
f
which solves the equation

\partial_{t}f+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x}f+\frac{1}{\kappa\varepsilon^{2}}Lf+\frac{(\partial_{t}+\varepsilon^{-1}\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})\sqrt{\mu}}{\sqrt{\mu}}f=g

(3.43)

for a general forcing
g
with the boundary condition
(
3.24
)
. In this section, we take the differential operator
\partial\in\{\varepsilon\partial_{t},\partial_{x_{1}},\partial_{x_{2}}\}
.
We define

\displaystyle d_{E}(t)=\frac{\kappa{\varepsilon}^{3}}{\delta}\left\|\textbf{NS}(U,P)\right\|_{\tau_{0},2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon\delta}\left\|{\rm div}U\right\|_{\tau_{0},2}+\frac{\kappa\varepsilon}{\delta}\||\nabla_{x}U\||_{\tau_{0},2}

(3.44)

\displaystyle+\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}\left\{\||\partial_{t}P+U\cdot\nabla_{x}P\||_{\tau_{0},\infty}+\varepsilon\||P(\partial_{t}U+U\cdot\nabla_{x}U)\||_{\tau_{0},\infty}+\||P\partial_{i}U_{j}\||_{\tau_{0},\infty}\right\}

\displaystyle+\frac{\varepsilon\kappa}{\delta}\left(\||(\partial_{t}+U\cdot\nabla_{x})(\partial_{i}U_{j})\||_{\tau_{0},2}+\varepsilon\||(\partial_{t}U+U\cdot\nabla_{x}U)(\partial_{i}U_{j})\||_{\tau_{0},2}+\||\nabla_{x}U\||_{\tau_{0},\infty}\||\nabla_{x}U\||_{\tau_{0},2}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}\||U\cdot\nabla_{x}U\||_{\tau_{0},2}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\delta}\||\partial_{t}P+\nabla_{x}\cdot(PU)\||_{\tau_{0},2}+\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3}\\
|\alpha|\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{\tau_{0}^{|\alpha|}}{\alpha!}\left\|\partial_{x_{3}}\partial^{\alpha}U\right\|_{L^{\infty}},

which will appear in the
L^{2}
analytic estimate of the Boltzmann solution.

Lemma 3.8
.

Fix
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{3}
. Then

b_{\alpha,3}=\mathcal{T}^{b}_{\alpha}-\varepsilon U_{3}a_{\alpha}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2\delta}\partial^{\alpha}\{U_{3}^{2}\}-\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}\partial^{\alpha}(PU_{3})

where

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\mathcal{T}^{b}_{\alpha}}{x_{3}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|f_{\alpha}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\sum_{\partial\in\{\partial_{x_{1}},\partial_{x_{2}}\}}\left\|\partial f_{\alpha}\right\|_{L^{2}}+(\varepsilon\delta)^{-1}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}(\nabla_{x}\cdot U)\right\|_{L^{2}}+\varepsilon\delta^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}(U_{i}^{2})\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle+\varepsilon^{2}\delta^{-1}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}\left(\partial_{t}P+\nabla_{\parallel}\cdot(PU_{\parallel})\right)\right\|_{L^{2}}+\delta^{-1}\varepsilon^{2}\kappa\left\|\partial^{\alpha}\left(\partial_{i}U_{j}(\nabla_{x}\cdot U)\right)\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta+\gamma=\alpha\\
|\gamma|\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{\alpha!}{\beta!\gamma!}\sum_{\partial\in\{\varepsilon\partial_{t},\partial_{x_{1}},\partial_{x_{2}}\}}\left\|\partial f_{\beta}\right\|_{L^{2}}\cdot\left\|e^{o(1)|\xi|^{2}}(1,\varepsilon\partial_{t},\nabla_{x})\partial^{\gamma}\sqrt{\mu}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}.

Proof.

We write

\displaystyle b_{3,\alpha}(t,x_{\parallel},x_{3})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{x_{3}}\partial_{3}b_{\alpha,3}dx_{3}^{\prime}.

Using the conservation law
(
3.39
)
, we get

\displaystyle\partial_{3}b_{\alpha,3}=-\varepsilon\partial_{t}a_{\alpha}-\nabla_{\parallel}\cdot b_{\alpha,\parallel}-\varepsilon\partial_{j}(U_{j}a_{\alpha})-(\varepsilon\delta)^{-1}\partial^{\alpha}({\rm div}U)-\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon\delta^{-1}\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{i}(U_{i}^{2})

\displaystyle-\varepsilon^{2}\delta^{-1}\partial^{\alpha}\left(\partial_{t}P+\nabla_{x}\cdot(UP)\right)

\displaystyle-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta+\gamma=\alpha\\
|\beta|\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{\alpha!}{\beta!\gamma!}(\varepsilon\partial_{t}+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})f_{\gamma}\cdot\partial^{\beta}\sqrt{\mu}d\xi-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta+\gamma=\alpha\\
|\beta|\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{\alpha!}{\beta!\gamma!}f_{\gamma}(\varepsilon\partial_{t}+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})(\partial^{\beta}\sqrt{\mu})d\xi

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{4}T_{\alpha,i}.

where

\displaystyle T_{\alpha,1}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon\sum_{j=1}^{2}\partial_{j}(U_{j}a_{\alpha})-\frac{1}{\varepsilon\delta}\partial^{\alpha}(\nabla_{x}\cdot U)-\frac{\varepsilon}{2\delta}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\partial^{\alpha}(U_{j}^{2})

\displaystyle-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\delta}\partial^{\alpha}(\partial_{t}P+\nabla_{\parallel}\cdot(PU_{\parallel}))+\delta^{-1}\kappa\varepsilon^{2}c_{ij}\partial^{\alpha}(\partial_{i}U_{j}{\rm div}U),

\displaystyle T_{\alpha,2}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta+\gamma=\alpha\\
|\gamma|\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{\alpha!}{\beta!\gamma!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(\varepsilon\partial_{t}+\xi_{\parallel}\cdot\nabla_{x_{\parallel}})f_{\beta}\cdot\partial^{\gamma}\sqrt{\mu}d\xi,

\displaystyle T_{\alpha,3}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta+\gamma=\alpha\\
|\gamma|\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{\alpha!}{\beta!\gamma!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f_{\beta}(\varepsilon\partial_{t}+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x})(\partial^{\gamma}\sqrt{\mu})d\xi,

\displaystyle T_{\alpha,4}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta+\gamma=\alpha\\
|\gamma|\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{\alpha!}{\beta!\gamma!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left\{\xi_{3}\partial_{x_{3}}\left(f_{\beta}\partial^{\gamma}\sqrt{\mu}\right)-\left(\xi_{3}f_{\beta}\partial_{x_{3}}\partial^{\gamma}\sqrt{\mu}\right)\right\}d\xi

\displaystyle-\varepsilon\partial_{x_{3}}(U_{3}a_{\alpha})-\frac{\varepsilon}{2\delta}\partial_{3}(\partial^{\alpha}(U_{3}^{2}))-\varepsilon\delta^{-1}\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{3}(U_{3}P).

As for
T_{\alpha,1}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|T_{\alpha,1}\right\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|f_{\alpha}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\sum_{\partial\in\{\partial_{x_{1}},\partial_{x_{2}}\}}\left\|\partial f_{\alpha}\right\|_{L^{2}}+(\varepsilon\delta)^{-1}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}(\nabla_{x}\cdot U)\right\|_{L^{2}}+\varepsilon\delta^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}(U_{i}^{2})\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle+\varepsilon^{2}\delta^{-1}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}\left(\partial_{t}P+\nabla_{\parallel}\cdot(PU_{\parallel})\right)\right\|_{L^{2}}+\delta^{-1}\varepsilon^{2}\kappa\left\|\partial^{\alpha}\left(\partial_{i}U_{j}(\nabla_{x}\cdot U)\right)\right\|_{L^{2}}.

Now for
T_{\alpha,2}
, we have

\left\|T_{\alpha,2}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta+\gamma=\alpha\\
|\gamma|\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{\alpha!}{\beta!\gamma!}\sum_{\partial\in\{\varepsilon\partial_{t},\partial_{x_{1}},\partial_{x_{2}}\}}\left\|\partial f_{\beta}\right\|_{L^{2}}\cdot\left\|e^{o(1)|\xi|^{2}}\partial^{\gamma}\sqrt{\mu}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}.

For
T_{\alpha,3}
, we have

\left\|T_{\alpha,3}\right\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta+\gamma=\alpha\\
|\gamma|\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{\alpha!}{\beta!\gamma!}\left\|f_{\beta}\right\|_{L^{2}_{x,\xi}}\left\{\left\|(\varepsilon\partial_{t})\partial^{\gamma}\sqrt{\mu}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}L^{2}_{\xi}}+\left\|\langle\xi\rangle\nabla_{x}\partial^{\gamma}\sqrt{\mu}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}L^{2}_{\xi}}\right\}.

For
T_{4,\alpha}
, using the fact that
U
vanishes on the boundary, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x_{3}}T_{\alpha,4}dx_{3}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta+\gamma=\alpha\\
|\gamma|\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{\alpha!}{\beta!\gamma!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\xi_{3}f_{\beta}\partial^{\gamma}\sqrt{\mu}d\xi

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta+\gamma=\alpha\\
|\gamma|\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{\alpha!}{\beta!\gamma!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{0}^{x_{3}}\xi_{3}f_{\beta}\partial^{\gamma}\left\{\left(\varepsilon\partial_{x_{3}^{\prime}}U\cdot\varphi\right)\sqrt{\mu}\right\}dx_{3}^{\prime}d\xi

\displaystyle-\varepsilon U_{3}a_{\alpha}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2\delta}\partial^{\alpha}\left\{U_{3}^{2}\right\}-\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}\partial^{\alpha}(PU_{3}).

Using the Hardy inequality, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{x_{3}}\int_{0}^{x_{3}}T_{\alpha,4}dx_{3}^{\prime}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta+\gamma=\alpha\\
|\gamma|\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{\alpha!}{\beta!\gamma!}\left\|f_{\beta}\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|e^{o(1)|\xi|^{2}}\partial^{\gamma}\sqrt{\mu}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}+\varepsilon\left\|f_{\alpha}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\varepsilon^{2}\delta^{-1}+\varepsilon\delta^{-1}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}(PU_{3})\right\|_{L^{2}}.

The proof is complete.
∎

Proposition 3.9
.

There holds
